No. l. 
Mr. Consul-General Cumberbatch to Viscount Palmerston.—( Received 
Seplember 7.) 
(No. 21.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, August 14, 1846. 


IN pursuance of instructions which I received from his Excellency 
Sir Stratford Canning, I have the honour to transmit to your Lordship 
the inclosed copy of a report which I addressed on the 14th instant to Her 
Majesty’s Minister Plenipotentiary at the Porte, on the several points in 
which the Tariff of 1889 may require amendment, and on the bearing of 
the existing commercial reel eone, upon British trade in Turkey. 

I have, &c., 
(Signed) A, CARLTON CUMBERBATCH, 


Consul-General, 


Inclogure 1 in No. 1. 
Mr. Consul-General Cumberbatch to the Hon. Hl. Wellesley. 


Sir, Constantinople, August 14, 1846. 

AGREEABLY to the instructions contained in a despatch circular, 
dated the 8th of June, of his Excellency Sir Stratford Canning, in which 
I am directed to furnish— 

Ist. Statements in detail respecting the several points in which the 
Tariff of 1839 may require amendment, with such suggestions for improve- 
ment as I may be able to obtain from British merchants within my 
district ; . 

2ndly. To furnish any further observations which I may have to 
offer at the present time respecting the bearing of the existing commercial 
regulations upon British trade with Turkey ; 

~ [have now the honour to state with reference to the first point, that 
the Tariff stands in need of a revision, in consequence of the great fall in 
the prices of almost all the principal articles of import from Great Britain, 
and of export from this country since that period. 

In 1839 the rates were fixed by the Commissioners upon the current 
srices of all the principal markets at the time when they were unusually 
ee and when they must have still been affected by the heavy and 
uncertain interior duties which were imposed upon them previous to the 
Convention of 1838. - 


4 


Since that period various causes have led to a reduction in the prices 
of almost every article of manufacture imported from Great Britain ; they 
may principally be ascribed to hens cheapness of production, the 
operation of the Convention, and the increased facilities of transport and 
communication by steam. ‘These causes being of a permanent nature, it 
is not to be expected that they will rise agatn. 

The prices of the principal articles of export have also fallen in an 
equal degree. 

I beg to inclose a copy of a letter which I have received from the 

rincipal British merchants of this city, to whom I applied for information 
in conformity with my instructions. 

Their report contains a statement of the prices of the present day of 
the principal articles of import and export, and of the prices upon which 
the Tariff rates upon them were fixed in 1838. 

Taking this statement as the basis of the calculation, it would appear 
that the rates payable upon the articles therein mentioned according to 
the existing Tariff, are upon an average 26 per cent. higher than they 
would be if calculated upon the present prices, as will be seen by the 
inclosed table, 

According to the Seventh Article of the Convention it is stipulated “that 
the Commissioners shall establish an equitable arrangement for estimating 
the interior duties which by the present Treaty are established on Turkish 
oods to be exported.” [t would appear, however, that the present 
‘ariff rates representing the 9 per cent. interior duties were calculated in 
the same manner as the rates which represent the 3 per cent. export 
duties; an arrangement which can hardly be deemed equitable as the rates 
of export duty are calculated upon the gross price of the merchandize at 
the ports of shipment, that is, with all the charges of carciage, profit of 
the seller, and other expenses upon the cost of the articles, which would 
not be included if in caleulating the rates for interior duty the provisions 
of the Fourth Article of the Convention were taken into consideration, 
which stipulates that merchandize shall be liable to the 9 per cent. duty 

on its entry into a convenient place of shipment. 

lt would be yery desirable if the commercial regulations of this 
country with the different foreign nations trading with it could be 
rendered uniform, and if one general Tarilf could be framed for all, 
commencing and ending at the same period, 

1 concur in the opinion of the merchants, that labour would be 
abridged, and the work more efficiently performed, if the Commissioners 
of the different nations which are about to revise their Tariffs were each 
to fix the rates upon the articles of import which more immediately 
interest their trade, and if they ae opeRies and formed the whole into a 
reneral Tarill; but it may be questionable whether their suggestion that 
the new Tariff should be made to extend until the expiration of the 
Russian Tariff would be advantageous to British interests. 

The Russian Tariff was to take effect from the 13th of October, 16-42, 
and was to last for twelve years from that period, Subsequently, by the 
late Treaty between Russia and the Porte, its duration has been extended 
until the expiration of the Treaty ; 1 will therefore not expire until April 
1856, instead of October 1854, as su yposed by the merchants. This dlif- 
ference in time is material, It has bean seen during the last seven years 
how much the prices of all the principal articles of trade have fallen; and 
taking into consideration the continual progress that is made in the pro- 
duction of British manufactures,—the rapidly-increasing facilities of 
transport,—as well as the measures which have passed the British Parlia- 
ment during the present session, it seems more than probable that the 
prices will still continue to fall. If this expectation be realized, the sooner 
the ‘Tariff is revised again, the more advantageous it will be for British 
interests, 

With respect to the second point upon which | am called to report,— 
namely, “the bearing of the existing commercial regulations upon British 
trade in ‘urkey,?—there can be no doubt that the effect of the Commer- 
cial Convention has been very beneficial, and that it has worked favour. 
ably for British trade, ‘The imports have pradually increased since 154%, 


“iliiees 


> 


{ 


3 


as the returns I herewith inclose will serve toshow. Since I entered upon 
the duties of my office on the Ist July 1845, | have only had cause for 
official interference in two complaints from British merchants within my 
district: one from Messrs. Hayes, Lafontaine, and Co., against the autho- 
rities of Augora, for levying, under the denomination of Iktissak, a duty 
upon goods purchased by their agents for exportation ; against which | 
protested, in conformity with my instructions. ‘The other, of Messrs. 

Thittall and Watson, against the Tanners of Seutari and the meven 
Towers, who on several oceasions interfered with agents employed by 
them for purchasing sheep-skins in the capital. ; 

In the first case, | understand that the authorities at Augora 
attempted to justify the exaction of the illegal duties, upon the grounds 
that they were levied upon the grower or seller, ad not upon the 
purchasers, 

In the second case, the Tanners maintained their exclusive right to 
purchase sheep-skins within the capital, in virtue of an ancient firman ; 
and although the Turkish authorities overruled this pretension in every 
instance upon which | had occasion to address them, still Messrs. Whit- 
tall and Watson were liable to continual molestation as long as the firman 
remained in existence. 

‘The withdrawal of this firman, and the exclusive privileges conferred 
by it—which you were pleased to communicate to me in your despatch of 
the 8th instant—will therefore remove any pretext for further molestation 
on the part of the Tanners with the purchasers of this article, 

The resolution of the Porte to take immediate measures to force the 
authorities at Augora to refund the Iktissab duties illegally levied on the 
eoods belonging to Messrs, Hayes, Lafontaine, and Co., it is to be hoped, 
when carried into execution, will have a desirable effect upon the autho- 
rities of that district; but as long as these and other interior duties are in 
existence, it is to be apprehended that complaints will arise. 

The lengthened persistance of the authorities af Augora, in maintain- 
ing the legality of these duties,—the difliculties the Porte appears to have 
SrpaRenec in enforcing its injunctions, have produced in these instances 
a delay which is incompatible with the interests of trade, 

It is to be observed that the principal trade between Great Pritain 
and Constantinople is in the bande of foreign and native merchants. 
Except in the neighbouring Broussa district, few purchases are made of 
produce by British merchants of this city im the interior, This may 
account perhaps for so few complaints having been made to me, 

The Iktissab and other existing interior duties have been lately 
prosiaimed by a Hatti-Sheriff of the Sultan to be oppressive, and are to 
ye partially abolished in the month of May next. . 

It is to be hoped that this partial measure will lead to their total 
abolition: that trade and agriculture may be relieved from the vexations 
and pressure which they occasion; and that the Porte, while exercising 
its rights of internal administration, will not suffer the existence of any 
duties hereafter which tend to counteract, either directly or indirectly, the 
privileges accorded to British subjects by the Convention, 

I have, &e., 
(Signed) A. CARLTON CUMBERBATCH, 
Consul-General,

Inclosuve 2 in No. 1. 
The British Merchants at Constantinople to Mr. Consul-General Cumberbateh. 


Sir, Galata, July 20, 1846. 

WE have respectively received your communication of the 9th instant, 
wherein it is stated that you had been instructed by Her Majesty's 
Ambassador to furnish statements in detail respecting several points in 
which the Tariff of 1839 may require amendment, and to that end you 
request us to report thereon. 


4 


This we take leave to do conjointly, rather than by separate answers 
to your inquiry, and transmit herewith 

Ist. A note showing the price at which the chief articles of British 
manufactures and twist were rated in the Tariff of duties of 1839, and of 
their present value in this market. 

2nd. A similar note of some of the leading articles of export, which 
clearly establishes the necessity of a revision of the Tariff; not, however, 
as exclusively applicable to those articles, but with reference to a general 
revision, in order that the Turkish authorities may have the benefit of any 
changes that may be found favourable to their interest. 

t seems to us very desirable in the revision about to take place, that 
(as every foreign Tariff is about to be revised, excepting that of Russia) 
the commissioners of each nation should confine themselves exclusively 
to such articles as more particularly interest the commerce of their respec- 
tive countries, as by this means their labours (as well as those of the 
Turks) would be greatly abridged and the work more efficiently per- 
formed, after which the whole might be formed into one general Tariff. 

We would venture further to suggest (as the Russian Tartif will 
expire in 1854, and our contemplated will terminate in 1853) how far it 
may not be desirable that arrangements be made for the extension of the 
period of our Tariff, so as to start in future from one fixed and uniform 
rate, 

As it appears inadmissible that we should, as we had previously 
suggested, be allowed to regulate our duties of customs subsequent to the 
expiration of the Tariff according to the rates about to fixed, we trust at 
least, that it may please our authorities to pees against any unnecessary 
delay that might arise on the part of the Turkish Government, which we 
bee the more reason to errchend from the fact of its being their interest 
to (Oo 80. 

We are at a loss to know by the terms of your inquiry how far our 
observations ought to extend; but it seems to us not irrelevant to the 
question to state, that we learn from the French Commissioners aireagy 
named for the revision of their Tariff, that they have been furnished with 
a copy of the new Russian Commercial Convention, and that it has served 
to guide them in their report on some points connected with a revision of 
their Variff, 

We have, &e., 
(Signed) T. R. BLACK, 
WOOD, JANIOU, & Coa, 
CHAS HANSON & Co, 
WHITTALL ann WATSON, 
HAYES, LAFONTAINE, & Co. 
CHAS. BEDE.

Inclosure 1 in No, 3. 
Mr. Consul-General Cumberbatch to the Hon. H, Wellesley. 


Sir, Constantinople, November 14, 1846. 


| HAVE the honour to inclose herewith a eopy of a letter which has 
been addressed to me by some of the Bnei British merchants of this 
city, in which they renew their request to be relieved from the pecs 
of the rates of the Tariff formed in 1839 in the manner propose® tin 
their letter of the 20th July, a copy of which I had the honour to trans- 
mit with my despatch of the L4th August last. ; oS 

T had Nately occasion to apply to the custom-house tn behalf of an 
lonjan merchant named G. ‘Tamvaco, who complained that his goods, 
consisting of British manufactures, had been retained until me cues 
upon them were settled according: to the rates of the Tarifl of I es had 
payinent of which he resisted upon the grounds that that 'Tarill hae 


9 


expired on the ygth March, and that it had not been determined what 
duties subsequently to that period, and until the revision of the new 
Tariff, ought to be charged. 

On that occasion the chief customer declared that he must continue 
to exact the duties according to the Tariff of 1839 in the same manner 
as was practised after the expiration of the previous Tariff in 1834, 
unless he received contrary instructions from the Porte. 

From the inquiries [have made among the British merchants who 
are principally concerned in the import trade, it does not appear that 
they have been called upon by the custom-house for some time past to 
settle their accounts, but they have been allowed to remain open for so 
longs time, that it may be expected that they will soon be required to 

0 50. 

it would be therefore desirable if an equitable arrangement could 
be effected on the subject with the Porte before that time. 

It would be very onerous to them if the practice which obtained at 
the expiration of the previous Tariff in 1834, be held as a precedent to be 
followed on the present occasion, and if the payment of the rates of the 
Tariff of 1889 were enforced until the indefinite period of the completion 
of the new Tariff; and in transmitting a copy of their letter, | therefore 
bee to recommend the auiee to your consideration, 

ave, &c., 
(Signed) A. CARLTON CUMBERBATCH,

Sir, Constantinople, November 11, 1846. 
ON the 20th July we had the honour of laying before you statements 


in pupet of the necessity of a revision of the Tariff of Duties which expired 
in March last. | 


_ We took leave at the same time to suggest, that Her Majesty's 
Government would be pleased to provide againt any unnecessary fae 
that might arise on the part of the arkish authorities, by fixing a berion, 
beyond which the rate of duties shall be taken, according to the Tariff 
yet to be formed, otherwise it might be expected that the Porte would 
seek to defer the renewal of it to an indelinite period. 

Nearly four months have expired, without our having any intimation 
of the intentions of Her Majesty's Government; meanwhile the British 
trade labours under oppressive duties, and therefore we again take the 
liberty of calling your attention to the subject.

Inclosure Lin No, 4. 


Mr. Consul Lander to the Hon, H. Wellesley. 


Sir, Dardanelles, September 8, 1846. 
| HAVE the honour of transmitting to your Excellency the annexed 
copy of a report which has this day been handed in to me by Mr. 
F. W. Calvert, in reply to the circular despatch addressed to him by 
his Excellency Sir Stratford Canning, imdee date of the Sth June 
last past, whilst in charge of the duties of this Consular office, during 
my late absence on Goyernment leave; and having satisfied myself as 
to the correctness of the information contained therein, | beg permis- 
sion to confirm its contents in every respect, and to recommend the 
amendments suggested by Mr. Calvert on various articles in the Tarill, 
as calculated to place British interests concerned in the export trade from 
these parts of Turkey, upon a more desirable footing than they now enjoy 
by the existing Tariff of 1839. 
I have, &c., 
(Signed) C. A. LANDER. 


Inclosure 2 in No. 4. 
Mr. Calvert to Mr. Consul Lander, 


Sir, Dardanelles, August 27, 1846, 
IN compliance with the instructions transmitted to me by his 
Excellency Sir Stratford Canning, by his despatch marked cireular, 
of 8th June last, | have the honour to wait upon you with the accom. 
panying statement of the different articles exported from this Consular 


jurisdiction, on whieh it is highly necessary for the interests of British 
merchants that the duties according to the TarilT of 1830 should be 
madified, 

| have not entered on the subject of imports, in consequence of all 
eoods arriving from the United Kingdom being brought from Constanti- 
nople or Smyrna, at which places the duties are paid. 


i 
i 


“= 


i 


The principal disadvantage with which the British trade had hitherto 
to contend, namely, the privileges enjoyed up to the present moment by 
the subjects of Russia, being removed by the Commercial Treaty recently 
conelnded between that country and the Porte, I consider the existing 
regulations as calculated in every way to fayour British commerce wit 
Turkey. 

I cannot, however, conclude without pointing out an undue advantage 
taken by Ottoman subjects of the Second Article, which provides for 
the protection of British subjects or their agents. Turkish subjects, 
though engaged in commercial transactions for their own account, as soon 
as they experience any difficulties, often find the means of getting a 
British or fonian subject to lend their names to operations which are 
essentially Turkish, and thus to obtain that Consular interference in their 
favour which they would not otherwise be entitled to. Such circum- 
stanees, to say the least, eannot fail to place Consuls in a disagreeable 
position towards the local authorities, who view in a disadvantageous 
ight the protection thus granted to one of their own subjects, 

| would therefore venture to suggest, for the purpose of remedying 
the evil as much as possible, that no Ottoman subject be entitled to 
Consular protection as agent for a British merchant, without being 

reviously duly constituted as such at the British Consulate, whose 
jurisdiction extends over the district in which he intends to carry on 
commercial operations ; 

That, after being thus constituted agent for a British merchant, the 
local authorities be officially informed of the fact; 

That all contracts or other engagements entered into between such 
an agent and the people of the country, be made out in the name of the 
menchene for whose account he is acting; and 

That any engagements contracted by the agent of a British merchant, 
previous to his having been thus duly recognized, as well as those made 
out in his own name, be not considered as British interests. 

have, &c., 
(Signed) F, W. CALVERT.

Inclosure 1 in No. 5. 


Mr. Consul Blunt's Report, contiimeng suggested amendments for the projected 
revision of the Tariff of 1839. 


IN transmitting the annexed list of the several articles of the Tariff 
of 1839, both of imports and exports, which appear to require amendment, 
also copies of the communications received from the British merchants 
established at Salonica upon the same subject, the Undersigned begs leave 
to state, in reference to those articles of import which are too highly rated 
in the ‘Tariff of 1839, it must be observed that the greater portion of 
British produce and manufactures imported are the low qualities. The 
only articles of export in the Tariff which, as far as regards the trade 
within the jurisdiction of this Consulate, are subject to too high an evalu- 
ation, are silks, tobaceos, wool, cotton, and olive oil. With respect to 
silk, the Undersigned has noted the different localities of production and 
rates OF average value, from which it will appear that the finer qualities of 
Salonica raw silks are undervalued, but that all raw silks from other 
towns within the jurisdiction of this Consulate are overvalued; it would 
therefore be more equitable if the finer qualities of Salonica raw silks were 
valued at 210 piastres per oke, and all the lower qualities, the produce of 
panes and other places named in the annexed list, at 140 piastres per 
oke. 

The high rates at which all the qualities, except one, of tobaceo, the 
produce of Roumelia,.are fixed in the Tariff of 1830, is a very serious tax 
upon the trade in that artiele; if, therefore, all the tobacecos from the 
districts named in the annexed list were valued at 24 piastres per oke, 
excepting the Yennigé Karrasst, which ought to be valued at. 6 piastres 
per oke, it would be more equitable. The Yenigé Karrassi and the 
Persofza tobaccos are very seldom exported; the greater part of these 
two latter qualities of tobacco are consumed in Turkey. Wools and 
cottons from Macedonia and Roumelia are not equal in quality to those 
from other parts of Turkey; hence the average value nent to be 
admitted instead of that at which the better qualities produced in other 
districts in the Ottoman dominions are rated. Olive oil produced within 
the jurisdiction of the Consulate of Salonica is from Volo, but is not equal 
in aunty to that from other places in Turkey; hence the average eyalua- 
tion ought to be admitted in lieu of classing the Volo oils with those of 
Mytelene, Ivalee, Candia, &c. 

___ ‘Phe prices of the different articles of export noted in the annexed list 
is the average of the last five years. 

With reference to the disadvantages accruing to the import trade of 
British manufactures at Salonica, owing to the high evaluation put upon 
the goods in the Tariff of 1839, subjecting the importer to the payment of 
from 7 to 10 per cent., instead of the 5 per cent. tinaludine the 2 per cent. 
internal duty) established by the Convention of Balta Liman, still the 
trade has, nevertheless, considerably increased in this part of Turkey 
since the period of its publication, as appears by the following note of 
imports from L840 ;— 


£ 
1840; : _ 22.000 
is4) | . 41,000 
Ist2 . . -WB165 
isis . 74,019 
isd i . _ 98,737 
iss . 182,817 


And the increase would have been to a greater extent, were it not for the 
construction which the Turkish authorities put upon the [[Lrd Article of 
the Convention, in reference to British manufactures. The interpretation 
which the Turkish authorities are pleased to give to this Article is, that 
all articles of cotton, silk, and wool manufacture, whether British or 


—— 


eS 


14 


Foreign, which are sold to Otteman subjects, and are in any way cut, 
stitched, or coloured, thereby become articles of Turkish manufacture, 
and are subject to a duty of 12 per cent. customs, and 2 per cent. Intisab, 
thereby subjecting British manufactures (since the ‘Turkish authorities 
pretend that British cotton manufactures are but raw materials) toa duty 
of 19 per cent. instead of 5 per cent. 

The Undersigned presumes lurther to add, that as the importers’ 
expenses (which latter observation may be admitted, to show the total 
weight of charges upon such gools as are brought within the construction 
which the Turks put upon the article in question) amount to 12% per cent., 
the total weight of charges and duty upon some kinds of British produce 
and manufactures before coming into the hands of the consumers, is 314 
per cent.; but in the interior, the evil is far greater, which is by no means 
surprising in a country like Turkey, which staggers under the weight of 
that barefaced system of corruption which is manifest in every branch of 
‘4g administration. Since this 3rd Article, with the construction which ts 
wut upon it, is availed of by the farmers of the customs and Intisab duties 
in the interior to the detriment of the internal trade, and there have been 
‘astanees in the more remote towns in the interior of the fiscal officers 
having levied a second duty of 12 per cent. customs and 2 per cent, 
Intisab upon these goods of Nurkish manufacture in the hands of the 
buyer! ‘This, however astounding it may appear, Is @ well-known faet, 
and here reported as an evidence of the oppressive system of corruption 
exercised by the minor authorities in the interior, and of the advantages 
taken of the construction put upon the 8rd Article in question. It would 
therefore appear that there really exists some secret understanding 
between the ‘Turkish Minister who sells the right of farming the Intisab 
and custom dues in the ‘nterior: for, as rewards the Intisab, the tariff 
which is delivered to the farmers of that duty makes no mention of any 
right to levy duties of any kind upon foreign or other manufactures that 
have already paid import duty; the Intisab tariff only allows that officer 
to levy a duty on shops, warehouses, and trades. 

The 3rd Article in question is adopted in the new Russian Convention, 
mn whieh the Sth Article isa copy, with the further most onerous addition 
that it is dependent upon the Gth Article of that Convention. 

The Undersizned ventures to hazard his liumble opinion with respect 
to this 8rd Article of the Convention of Balta Liman, that it is of the 
utmost importance to British trade in Turkey, that it should be more 
clearly defined; the Article states, © and in any manner of trade therein 
the same duties that are paid in similar circumstances by the most favoured 
class of ‘Turkish subjects engaged in the internal trade of ‘Turkey, 
whether Musulmans or Rayas.” It depends much therefore for Her 
Majesty’s Consuls in ‘Turkey to inquire and know who are the more 
favoured classes of Musulmans and Rayas? in what consist the immuni- 
ties which render them in a commercial sense so favoured, that the British 
merchant would find it to his advantage to avail himself of the exemptions 
which they enjoy? The Article may probably refer to Barathes, @ cliss 
of Ottoman subjects who purchase a herat, which gives them the right of 
paying duties the same as Franks, and of settling their commercial dis- 
putes at the custom-house, or of referring them to Constantinople. 

There is certainly another class of favoured individuals in ‘Turkey, 
who obtain the right of monopolies and the farming of duties at a very 
low rate of purchase, and who, upon the strength of that favour, tax the 
Rayas in the interior with the most exorbitant dutics. These are the 
only class of favoured Ottoman subjects, whether Musulman or Rayas, 
that the Undersigned ever heard of, after a residence of twenty-seven 
years in Turkey. a 

The Undersigned humbly begs to call the attention of Her Majesty’s 
Government to the 5th and ih Articles of the Russian Convention, the 
which, if adopted, would not only be the means of driving thousands of 
British-protected subjects out of ‘Turkey, but would also strike a most 
ruinous blow at the British import trade. Few importers would be able 
to run off their stocks if the provision of the 6th Article were to be 
enforced, most of the buyers from the interior make their regular trips ta 


15 


this A as they do all large commercial town in Turkey, and select 
small lots of goods, some of them taking perhaps only one or two pieces 
of each article, and a few bundles of the ditterent Kinds of twist, and this 
it may be added is the best trade, as they are all cash payments - if then 
the importer 18 deprived of this advantage he is entirely thrown upon the 
hands of the esnaffs or trades (for all the different trades are termed 
esnaffs), and thereby reduced to accepting the onerous terms of long credit 
offered by the esnatfs. 
_ “Phe Undersigned begs leave to state, with reference to the 4th Article 
in the Russian Convention, that it is usual with the importers at Salonica 
to pay 4 per cent, upon their goods in the first instance when imported 
when Aine he customer willingly accepts, instead of depending 
upon the uncertainty of receiving the 2 per cent. addition: iy : 
sale of the goods ioe the itecon” haa ae ia 
The annexed report of Messrs. Abbott Brothers refers to the dervent 
dues, and complains of such duties having been levied upon grain pur- 
chased by these gentlemen or their agents; but they have omitted to 
state that these duties of which they complain were levied upon their 
Boos prior to April 1845, at which period his Excellency Sir Stratford 
anning obtained a most categorical order from the Porte prohibiting the 
levy of this duty upon British property and since the reception of this 
order at Salonica upon the sale of these duties it is always stipulated 
(which is inserted in the bouyourdi) that no dervent dues are to be levied 
upon British property ; hence since April 1845, the Undersigned has had 
but few applications of any infringement of this order by the farmers of 
the dervent dues ; and when such application has been made the amount 
taken has invariably been reriened Messrs. Chasseaud and Vatliard 
make no mention of these dervent dues. sf 
That the dervent dues are a heavy tax upon the internal trade in the 
hands of the native merchant there can be no doubt, more so probabl 
that the Porte is aware of; and it is also clear that such dues which se 
arbitrarily levied upon Ottoman subjects, become a heavy tax upon all 
kinds of goods going to and coming from the interior. 

With respect to the assertion advanced by Messrs. Abbott Brothers 
relating to the officers of the custom-house of one Turkish port not 
acknowledging the validity of the teskéré or permit of the customs of 
another Turkish port, the Undersigned has no knowledge of any British 
subject within the jurisdiction of Her Majesty’s Consulate at Salonica, 
hay nee bad sane to complain of this infraction of the Convention of

Inclosure 3 in No. 5. 


Messrs. Chasseaud & Co. to Mr. Consul Blunt. 
Sir, Salonica, September 28, 1846, 
IN reply to your despatches of the 19th of June and of the 7th 
instant, by which you make us known the desire of Her Majesty's 
Ambassador at the Ottoman Porte to be furnished by us with statements 
respecting the different points in which the Tariff of 1839 may require 
amendment; we join to hand annexed prospectus of average prices, 
upon which should be taken the basis of stipulating the duties in the 
reform of a new tariff. 

Between the great wants in the existing Tariff, we beg to note one 
which is of great consideration, namely, that the commerce should have 
the prerogative to pay in nature the custom-house duty from the article 
itself, though tariffed, in case of disagreement, 

On many articles of produce of the Ottoman Empire, the Government 
receives besides the duty specified in the Tariff, an extra duty from the 
seller; which of course falling upon the merchandize, renders the price 
of the same very heavy. It would be therefore highly desirable that the 
Turkish Government should not be allowed to aggravate the cultivators 
with any further duty, under any denomination whatever, besides the 
dime and the Tariff duty. 

We dare hope that Her Majesty’s Government will further take into 
consideration, in stipulating a new Tariff, not to put ber subjects under 
those of any favoured nation, 

We have, &e., 
(Signed) HENRY CHASSEAUD, 
STEFO, VAFFIARDY & CO,

Her Majesty’s subjects engaging in any species of internal trade in 
Turkish products, are usually called upon to pay only the duties attached 
to the articles in which they deal. In rare cases, as that of carrying on 
the business of a licensed wine tavern of which the number is limited here 
to 13, they would further have to discharge an annual tax of about 101, 
the same as rayas. 

Their right to manufacture products of the country, I have been 
instructed is doubtful. But in regard to making candles, it was admitted 
by a former Governor here, one of the Commissioners who signed our 
Convention of 1838, to be in conformity ; and the Turkish authorities do 
not, without the consent of the British. assume the power of preventing: its 
exercise, 

There has been no opposition to a “ Filature” of silk on the French 
model, established and now much extended by the Russian Vice-Consul, 
who pays the customary tithe on the product, and a small tax on each 
reel employed, the same as levied on those of the country. 

The local advantages of such enterprises of Europeans, are obvious in 
the expenditure of capital and introduction of improvements, 

In general, however, I find the Turkish authorities latterly object 
more to British subjects carrying on any sort of trade interfering with 
that of their Esnafs, comprising the different bodies of shopkeepers, 
artisans, and others of almost every avocation, on the Freund of the com- 
petition being detrimental to the revenue, by diminishing the gains of 
those who pay taxes. 

lt appears that Russia by the new Treaty with the Porte, published 
in the semi-ollicial Turkish paper, has amongst other restrictions agreed to, 
consented to renounce this privilege for her subjects: of these, however, 
[am assured there is scarcely one born in her dominions, and not in her 
ollicial service residing in other parts of Turkey more than this, whose 
interests can he affected in consequence. 

On account of the thousands of British and lonian subjects settled in 
the various districts, such repulations could searcely be carried into effect 
without causing a great disturbance of individual capital or industry 
resting on the protection of prescriptive usage and treaty. 

The alteration would go to preclude them from the advantages of 
vending in detail our manufactures particularly in the same localities as 
the native retailers, themselves often importing traders also. Therefore 
without such reciprocal facility for our merchants, combined with their 
wholesale business they would not be on a full footing of equality, And 
I was assured by the chief of a Glasgow import house, who visited this 
seme years ago, that such was the only method of entering into successful 
competition with the traders of the country, as practised by establishments 
with which he was connected in other parts of the Mediterranean, 

Here, as probably elsewhere, in case of distinctions of locality being 
made, questions connected with it might probably arise, not easy of 
ndjustment, 

The taxes or dues payable by native subjects, incidental to the exer- 
cise of their trades are very inconsiderable in amount, as an object in case 
of being applicable to British subjects under the same circumstances. 
And as they are always free to sell their goods in any quantity or mode 
found convenient in their usual store, the restrictive remedy sought could 
al most be partial, 

There is a distinct trafic here of some amount shared by lonians, in 
oil, soap, and riee, imported for local consumption, which are occasionally 
anicea for sale in the open market, although not without opposition 
having been made to the practice. 

It renders them less dependent on the offers of the native shopkeepers 
who have the exclusive privilege amongst Turkish subjects of Seuuline 
these articles: but at taxed rates for each ordered by their authorities, 
without distinction of quality. It is in consequence furnished by them 
most frequently adulterated, or of an inferior description, against which 
the only protection for the consumers is the recourse at their option to 
foreign vendors. | 


In respect of working trades, xs those of carpenters, tailors and the 


SS 


26 


like now free, their being wholly prohibited to strangers would render all 
foreipn residents in mares dependent for various wants on native artisans 
caneule only for the most part of furnishing inferior articles or workman- 
ship, besides the prejudice arising to those deprived of their employment. 
Notwithstanding the engagement for the abolition of all monopolies, 
one has been retained by the Government for the manufacture and sale of 
snull, from tobacco of the country, which is farmed out to parties vested 
with Lhe exclusive privilege. any 
Quantities of it ceded by them, have come into the hands of British 
subjects here, the resale of which alter encountering oecasional objections, 
has alwavs been allowed to proceed. ne: oP 
The Russian Treaty cited, concedes to the Sublime Porte the additional 
monopoly of foreign snuff as regards its retail, and places under special 
restrictions the trade in various other articles left hitherto perfectly free 
in this quarter. ; o | 
With the reservations mentioned, this freedom extends to all exports 
and imports, in accordance with the tenor of the British Convention of 1535, 
without exception or reference to circumstances. And the principle has 
proved to be the best, if not alone ealeulated to ensure a permanent and 
regular system of duties and full exchange of commodities hetween the 
two countries. 
The Treaty was framed to correct the previous abuses and irregu- 
larities in this respect and prevent their renewal, ashe 
Its virtue is liable to be entirely impaired by the retention on the part 
of the Sublime Porte, of the power of prohibiting at diseretion the expor- 
tation of any Turkish prea nee as exemplilied by experience; and a re: 
approach to the exploded system of pro ibitions, monopolies, and par~ 
tial protections, would assuredly prove injurious to the real interests of 
this part of the country In common w ith those of our trade. | 
Untoward seasons and onerous duties now in the course of repeal, 
arrested its growing progress here, so as not to exhibit fully that increase 
of production which has been the general result of the more perlect com- 
mercial freedom established by our last Treaty, and the best evidence of 
its advantares. Lehee at 
Its provisions, from their concise and liberal tenor, are of easy apphiea- 
tion, whilst possibly in some points susceptible of alteration or improve- 
ment; and a considerable increase of the duties payable was stipulated 
jointly with any extension of the immunities of British subjects and trade 
in ‘Turkey, whilst the regulations were common to Ottoman subjects,

Inclosure in No, 8. 


Mr. Consul-General Colquhoun to Sir Stratford Canning. 


Sir, Bucarest, July 3, 1846, 
_ | HAVE the honour to acknowledge receipt of your Excellency’s 
circular of the 8th June respecting the working of the Parift of 1839, and 


requiring me to state wherein it may need ar us re 
Principality of Wallachia y need amendment as regards the 

_On this subject I have very little to remark; a reference to my pre- 
ceeding despatches of 1839, 1840, and subsequently, will show that the 
Principalities never availed themselves of the increased duties offered by 
the Treaty, as in fact the object for which the Treaty was made, viz., to 
do away with monopolies, did not exist here; they maintained their old 
3 per cent. duties on the ad valorem price of all articles, whether imported 
or exported; in the latter case the rate was fixed for the current five 
years on the mean price of the preceding five years, the customs being 
farmed for that period of time. The said rate in most cases rather 
exceeded in amount 3 per cent., though there are some few instances in 
which it was less than 3 per cent. 

There is one question to which I have drawn attention wherein the 
Wallachian Government refuses positively to conform to the Treaty—that 
of the right of warehousing at Ibraila the produce of Turkey. (Vide No, 18 
of 24th September 1839, and No. 10 of 17th April 1843, &e,) This 
question, so Important to the merchant residing at Galatz or Ibraila, but 
purchasing produce in Bulgaria, has remained unmooted, perhaps from 
the real position of the provinces as regards their sovereign court, but 
this of course involves a question of difficult solution, , 

_ The only other question likely to cause embarrassment, but which 
believe has not yet occurred, is that of the refusal of the Wallachian 
sovernment to allow articles of foreign produce to enter the province 
without paying the 3 per cent., although they may have already paid the 
4 per cent. in another port of Turkey. ‘This and the right of preventing 
the exportation of grain in cases of scarcity, as recently mentioned by me 
the Government claims in virtue of its Réglement Organique. These are 
the only points on which I have anything to remark in reply to your 
Exeellency's letter, and which I beg to submit to your consideration. 
[ have, &c., 


(Signed) ROBERT G. COLQUHOUN,

I have the honour to state that the Treaty in question has never 
been applicd to this ETivelaaley, which has a separate administration 
sanctioned by the Turkish Government. The former Tariff of Turkey 
has been long followed for the import and export commerce, all merchan- 
dize paying 3 per cent. ad valorem, on being disembarked at Galatz, or on 
entering the countey by the frontier-customs from Austria or Russia 
with 2 per cent, additional for home consumption, and a general rate of 
duty of 3 per cent, is paid on the produce of the country exported, with 
the two important exceptions for corn and cattle, which pay a fixed duty

Inclosure 2 in No. 10. 


Report on the Tariff of 1839, as regards the British Trade of Moldavia, Wallachia, 
and Bulgaria on the Danube. 


MOLDAVIA. 


THIS province not having availed itself of the new Treaty of 1839 
which enabled the Government to raise the duties to 12 per cent. on 
articles of exportation, and 5 per cent. on articles of importation: that 1s, 
on exportations 9 per cent of internal duty when articles were purchased, 
and 3 per cent. when they were exported from the province, and on 
articles of importation 3 per cent. at the time of importation and 2 per 
cent, more on their being sold for consumption; but having kept to the 
rate of duties levied before the date of the new Treaty, which in general 
is 3 per cent. ad valorem on all articles imported and exported, there is 
no aianee on the Tariff called for. 

The exceptions to the rate of 3 per cent. are on articles of 


Evportation. 
Money of Visteria. Currency of Galatz. Hate percent. on 
jaatres. Piastres. Medium Price.

Barley, ditto 2 6 6 2 2 3. us SO ye 
Indian Gorn, ditto .. 240 . .. 883 . . 5 .

Tobaceo, peroke . . 2 piastres . . 20 per cent. 


The Moldavian Government however on one point refuses to give force 
to the Treaty of Commerce of 1839, namely Articles LV, as the importa- 
tion of wheat and all kinds of grain and tallow, the produce of Turkey, 
cannot be introduced into Moldavia for the purpose of exportation. 

It may also be remarked on the Article LV of the Treaty, that it does 
not go far enough, as it should be clearly expressed that ‘all goods 
pure iased in Turkey for exportation, and which have paid full duties 
) per cent. and 3 per cent. may be carried from one shipping port to 
another or more, and be shipped from thence without any further duty 
ever being demanded.” 

In Moldavia Government pretends to have the right under the 
“Réglement Organique,” to prohibit the exportation of all kinds of 
provisions, so often as they may fear a scarcity. 

Galatz is a free port in some respects, that is in so far that all 


articles not expressly prohibited may be imported and re-exported 
without paying any duty whatever. 


WALLACHIA. 


The Government of Wallachia did not change the rate of duties in 
consequence of the new ‘Treaty of 1839, but kept to the former rate of 3 
per cent. on importations and exportations. 


'The fixed rates on the exportation of grain from Wallachia, are,— 


Pinstres. Ou Medium Price, 


Wheat, perkilo . . . 315 


3 per cent. 
Rye, ditto: >. a te USBI Are 4 8 a

Barley, ditto 2 2. ee PD & 2 ee a

The articles of which the importations into Wallachia are altogether 
prohibited, are wheat, Indian corn, tallow and salt. 

The Wallachian Government claim the right of prohibiting all kinds 
of provisions, so often as they fear a scarcity ; and acting on this claim of 
right, it in January last laid on an additional duty on all kinds of grain, 
of 24 piastres per kilo ; this has caused a serious loss to foreign merchants 
who held grain. The right to prohibit the exportation of an article in an 
extreme case cannot be construed into that of laying an arbitrary tax to 
the prejudice of foreign merchants. A merchant does not care so much 
for the amount of a tax as for the certainty that he will not be called on 
to pay more than what is fixed. ; 

Ibraila is a free port to the same extent as Galata. 

It must be further remarked that when British produce is imported 
from Turkey either into Moldavia or Wallachia, even when it may have 
paid full duties of 5 per cent, in Turkey, and come accompanied with 
proper teskérés, still the duty of 3 per cent. must be again paid in the 
province. 


BULGARIA. 


The chief articles of exportation from Bulgaria on the Danube being 
grain, it is necessary to observe here that the Tariff of 1839 is rather 
high. 


Aspers. 
Wheat, from Turkey. : 150 48 
Barley, ditto. +. + ; 67 23 
[Indian corn, ditto. ; ; 75 25 


W heat is therefore valued at piastres 13 35 per kilo, but the customers 
in Bulgaria only demand piastres 1 12 of duty, thus valuing the wheat at 
piastres 10 85; thus although the valuation 1s just as concerns Constan- 
tinople, it is high as regards Bulgaria. . 

The trade of Bulgaria on the Danube seems to go on satisfactorily, 
but is kept back from the want of a general place of depét and shipping 
port, but this in time will find a remedy. | 

In conclusion, there does not appear to be any change required either 
in the Tariff or Treaty for the trade of the Danube. 

(Signed) CHARLES CUNNINGHAM, 
. Vice- Consul.

Inclosure 1 in No. 11. 
Mr. Consul Saunders to Sir Stratford Canning. 


British Consulate in Albania, 
Sir, Yonina, August 17, 1846. 

IN reply to your Excellency’s despatch marked cireular of the 8th 
of June, requiring me to furnish statements in detail respecting the several 
points in which the Tariff of 1839 may re uire amendment, | have the 
honour to acquaint your Excellency in the first place as re ards articles 
of import, that the principal objections which have hitherto Been found to 
exist in the application of the Tariff valuations, have been compromised 
in this province by the eustom-house consenting of late years, in lieu of 
the prices set forth in the Tariff, to take the invoice value of the goods as 
the basis of their computation of import duties, which the reduction of 
of prices rendered a desirable alleviation to the importer. 

lt may be scarcely necessary therefore that l should extend my 
remarks further upon this portion of the Tariff, respecting which your 
Excelleney may derive more practical observations from other quarters. 

With regard to the export duties on the produce of this country, I 
have the honour to annex a list of those articles which from their high 
rate of valuation in the Tariff of 1839 may require some diminution in the 
established duties, or at all events may be advantageously left to pay a 
duty of 12 per cent, upon the market price. 

Upon this point I further beg to annex extract of a report on the 
working of the Commercial Convention of 1838 which I forwarded in 1842 
to Her Mae Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs, stating the com- 
plaints which had been made to his Excellency the Lord High Commis- 
sioner in the Ionian Islands, of the high rate of the export duty levied in 
this province on wool, and the arrangement subsequently effeeted for a 
partial reduction thereof, as well as for a diminished rate of export duty 
on valonea, whereon the Tariff rates also proved inordinately high. 

These reductions, however, made by special agreement with the 
farmers of the customs’ duties for two or three years, were scareely main- 
tainable last year, and are not to be expected during the present one; 50 
that unless it be deemed convenient for the future to allow these articles 
to pay an ad valorem duty, it would be highly desirable that some distine- 
tion should be made between the respective qualities of each, instead of 
one and the same rate of duty being levied on wool, “fine and course,” 
and yalonea “all sorts; or finally, if such distinction be considered likely 
to prove a fertile source of contention, some reduction of the present 
‘Tariff rates on these articles may be fairly contended for, ‘The first of 
these arrangements however may be considered the most beneficial, 
and was made applicable to both these articles in the Russian ‘Tariff of 
842. 

In conformity with your Excelleney’s desire that I should furnish at 
the present time any further observations which I may have to offer 
respecting the bearing of the existing eommercial regulations upon 
British trade with Turkey, | avail myself of this opportunity to offer some 
remarks upon a communication? which has been made public, whereby it 
would seem that the Turkish Government, under the pretext of a revision 
of the Commercial Tariffs, at the same time wishes to infringe upon the 
provisions of the Convention itself, by calling anew into existence some of 
those monopolies which it was one of the principal objects of that Con- 
vention to abolish. 

The stipulations upon this head are so precise that no possibility of 
mistake ean arise as to their unlimited application to all articles without 
any exception whatsoever, the produce, growth, or manufacture of the 
said (Ottoman) dominions ;"" which articles it is provided “the subjects 
of Her Britannic Majesty, or their agents, shall be permitted to purchase 
at all places in the Ottoman dominions, whether for the purpose of internal 
trade or exportation.”

Ottoman Empire all kinds of merchandize, and also to resell or to export 

the merchandize so purchased, nothing remains to be desired in this pro- 
vince as regards complete freedom of action in all matters of trade to 
which British subjects may choose to direct their attention. :

With respect to the first, which treats of the Public Fisheries, no pos- 
sible pretension to interfere therewith could ever have been experienced so 
as to render necessary any stipulation of the kind; and the mere sale of 
fish as a matter of internal commerce is of course governed by the local 
restrictions affecting that or any other produce of the country, whatever 
those restrictions may happen to be. 

The fishing for Leeches, which is next adverted to, is also a privilege 
that has always remained in the hands of the Government, and as such is 
farmed out from year to year in the several districts; so that it cannot be 
argued that the stipulations of the British Convention are in any way 
found to affect the same. The purchase of leeches for exportation is quite 


another matter, which is not called in question even in the Russian 
Treaty, 


The third head contemplates a monopoly less objectionable perhaps 
in its application as the burden must fall upon the native consumer, but 
the more obnoxious in principle from Alum being in most parts of Turkey 
an article of import acd not of export. It is expressly comprised as such 
in the British Import Tariff annexed to the Convention of 1838, and 
nowhere appears in the list of exports. The special provision therefore 
made for the liberty to purchase this article for exportation, which (as in 
the foregoing case of leeches) might have been left to be inferred as a 
matter of course, would appear to have been devised by the Ottoman nero- 
Hator as a blind to cover the unwarrantable interference with the 
mparted article,

consist in the payment to the farmer of this appalto of a special impost, 
namely, three piastres per oke. "The pretension, therefore, to form in this

produce of the country, is wholly uncalled for, and would be a gratuitous 
interference with the provisions contained in the Second Article of the 
British Convention.

prejudiced ; for 


import trade; and thus both Turkey and Great Britain suffer, 


t a f # i =. re oe ; Th . & Wy ’ t ‘ 
In considering this question, it is requisite to bear in mind what th 
present Tariff replaced 


-—one much lower, it is true, but at the same time 


37 


accompanied by a system of monopolies which thwarted all calculation, 
and rendered every transaction uncertain, besides in most cases occasion- 
ing higher duties than are now paid. I am unhesitatingly of opinion 
that the present state of things is much to be preferred to the old. The 
Turkish Government does not consider the Tariff advantageous to the 
revenue; and I am inclined to believe that a return to the old system 
would be acceptable to it, But the producers, | think, are better con- 
tented that the matter remain as at present; and it would be dangerous 
to insist on a change, unless a sufficient guarantee were given that mono- 
polies should not be revived. The Porte depends for its revenue, in great 
part, on the duties on commerce; and it could not afford to risk a reduc- 
tion in the amount they bring in,—for it would be nearly impossible to 
substitute any other tax. The direct taxes are already so numerous and 
so burdensome, and they give rise to so much injustice and vexation, that 
it would be the height of impolicy to add to their number or to increase 
theiramount. Whether the reduction of duties on exports, by production 
and exportation, would augment the revenue, is doubtful; for there are 
so many causes in existence which would counteract the beneficial effect 
of such reduction. Still, | cannot but think that if an uniform duty of 
5 per cent. were adopted in all cases, to be levied on the exporters and 
importers at the ports of embarkation and landing, abolishing every kind 
and denomination of inland duty, it would tend very much to the eneou- 
ragement of commerce and to the convenience and relief of all classes of 
traders and producers. Were this system adopted, and were custom- 
houses laced only on the coast and on the frontier, the collection of the 
revenue would be simplified, peculation would be prevented with greater 
facility, and the expense of collection would be diminished; and these 
advantages could not but have a tendency to increase the revenue, But, 
to carry this out, all European nations must agree to accept the same 
Treaty and Tariff; for without this unanimity, it were vain to attempt 
any such plan. 

The venality of Turkish employés is notorious, and all the regulations 
introduced and orders given to arrest. this evil, have produced no sensible 
effect: and as it is of course more difficult to check it, the greater 
the number of employés, to diminish them is to facilitate the end 
desired, 

It is as notoriously true as to be lamented, that from the want of 
enlarged views in Turkish statesmen, and from the venality and bad faith 
of employés generally, no great trust can be placed in liberal stipulations 
being observed, unless Treaties are very precise and stringent. It were 
most desirable that such precise stipulations were made in our Treaties 
with regard to internal trade; for if this be not provided for, a way will 
be found of eluding the benefits conceded to Europeans, by taxing internal 
trade, Numerous instances of this unfair dealing can no doubt be cited 
in various parts of Turkey. As this is not a country producing export- 
able commodities, | have none to quote; but | may mention one striking 
example, namely, the silk of Amassia, which is charged with a very heavy 
duty before it is brought to market, and afterwards with the 12 per cent, 
of the Tariff. I may cite also the duty of Intissab, which is still levied 
in many places, although it was considered as abolished by the Conven- 
tion of 1838, 

[ have in the preceding part of this despatch, shown that the high 
duties on the productions of Turkey limit the import trade; and this 
action is what is most prejudicial to Great Britain. It is of great import- 
ance to her to find free markets for her manufactures, and to encourage 
By every means the expansion of those markets. Such a one exists in 

urkey, and its extension would follow the increased demand for Turkish 
produce, likely to arise from lower duties, and with the more certainty, if 
the agriculturists were encouraged to augment production, by freedom 
from the vexatious and illegal demands on them to which they are sub- 
ject. ‘This security is only to be hoped for by the Porte keeping a more 
effectual check oyer provincial governors; and if their own employés 
are so universally steeped in venality as to play into each other's hands, 
then a substitute may be found through the agency of European Consuls, 


=e 


38 


which would be efficient, if the Porte itself were disposed to act on authen- 
tic information, sought and communicated through the channel of foreign 
Embassies. This sort of interference may be considered objectionable ; 
and it cannot be denied that it has inconveniences. But in the state of 
information in Turkey, without extraneous influence, the Government 
would searce know how to adopt a right course, or, if adopted, would 
mm continue it, 

BON Gees new commercial regulations be made, they should be 

neral, as well for all European nations as for every class of natives, 
By that means alone, could they be expected to be either effective or 
jérmanent. Partial modifications would do no material good, either to the 
Suropean or Turkish Governments; but one general and liberal system 
would be most assuredly beneficial to both parties. 

I have, &c., 
(Signed; JAS. BRANT.

| HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Excellency’s 
circular dated the 8th instant, desiring me to furnish your Excellency 
with statements respecting several points in which the Tariff of duties 
established in 1839 may require amendment, with any suggestions which 
British merchants residing here have to offer, connected with its improve- 
ment, and, moreover, any remarks I may consider it necessary to make 
respecting the bearing of the existing commercial regulations upon our 
ade in this country. wt 
ee " laid your Bxcelleney'a circular before the British merchants. ‘The 
only point which they, as well as myself, deem deserving of MB ESGINEH 
is the duty now levied on British cotton manufactures, in virtue of the 
sent ‘Tariff, 
a “When the Tariff! was established, the 3 and 2 per cent. duties on 
British cotton manufactures were calculated on their estimated value, 
Since that period, British cotton goods have fallen in value at least a 
per cent., so that if the present value of our cotton oods be taken, ane 
the above-mentioned rates of duty be calculated thereon, it will result 
that the amount now paid equals 8 per cent. | . 
Trebizond being almost destitute of an export trade, neither the 
British merchants nor I have any suggestions to offer respecting: it. | 
Great inconvenience is experienced by our merchants, in consequence 
of a refusal on the part of the customs at Constantinople, to allow the 
duty on merchandise imported by them for sale to be paid at the custom- 
house here. That refusal obliges traders to employ agents at the 
apital for settling the duty, previous to the goods coming hither. ‘That 


be well furnished from the ports where 
duties. 


og 


agent charges a per centage on the transaction; he is frequently obliged 
by the Constantinople custom-house to open his constituents’ goods, 
which get ill-treated, and I have even heard complaints made by the 
merchants, that articles have been extracted from the packages by the 
custom-house employés, whilst the goods are being inspected. “This 


detriment and vexation would be spared to the trader, were he allowed 
to settle the duty here. 

The British merchants of Trebizond have no further suggestions to 
offer respecting the amendment of the Tariff of 1839, with the working of 
which they express themselves satisfied.

copy of this communication to the Foreign Office.

The Tariff specifies some imports and exports upon which, as well as all 
others not therein enumerated, the duties are to be levied ad valorem, at 
the per centage rates stipulated by the Commercial Convention. Under 
the system adopted by the Porte of farming its customs, these duties are 
complained of as leading to much dispute and delay in mercantile trans- 
actions; for the customers of course always endeavour to exact them upon 
as high a price as possible. This frequently is attempted at exorbitant 
valuations; and I have always heard it argued as desirable, that the 
inconvenience be obviated by an option being secured to the merchant, 
of giving ad valorem duties in kind in cases where an equitable arrange- 
ment regarding them cannot be at once effected with the customer. 
With reference to the second part of your Kxeellency 


’s instructions, | 
ean only say that all the information | am able to obtain, and the result 


of my) own observation, induce me to believe that the bearing of the 
existing commercial regulations is favourable to British trade with 


Turkey, and that they would prove still more so with the amendments I 
have ventured above to bring to your Excellency’ 


8 notice, and were some 
of the stipulations of the Commercial Convention which have been clis- 


regarded by ‘Turkish authorities required to be fully adhered to, 

I have from time to time been under the necessity of representing to 
your Excellency the revival of some monopolies and various irrerular 
exactions in this direction. The orders your Exeellency was pleased to 
procure from the Grand Visier, removed Jast year, so far as it applied 
to British merchants, the prohibition to free trade in salt, wines, spirits, 
gunpowder, lead, leadshot, alum and snuff, which had been re-estab. 
lished at Tarsous; but I am reeently informed by Mr. Vice-Consul 
Clapperton, that the authorities there have intimated their intention 
of renewing those restrictions, without any exception in favour of 
foreign traders. The toll called ‘: Batch,” of 8 to 5) plastres per load, 
levied on merchandise passing to and fro through the Koulek Boghaz 
defile ofthe Taurus, and that under the denomination of Yioklama,” of 


3 piastres per load, exacted on imports at Kaissar 


. lah, are in consequence 
of the orders obtained by your Excellency from the Porte, discontinued 


to be demanded from British merchants. They are still taken however 
from the native traders, notwithstanding their merchandise be accom- 
panied by certificates proving the payment of all regular cuties, and con- 
sequently British and other European products and manufactures thus 
continue indirectly subjected to these undue charges. ‘The levy of 
* Yioklama,’’* Batch,” Derbend dues,” and tolls under other names, is 
practised in many parts of Anatolia. The charge is ostensibly made “ to 
defray the expense of guards and other arrangements to insure the secure 
passage of caravans through particular districts where danger from 
robbers may be apprehended.” In no instance however that {| am aware 
of, is the produce of these tolls applied to such a purpose, nor does the 
risk against which they purport to provide, exist in places where many of 
them are taken, The right of levying them which is sold at Constanti- 
nople, or in the proyinees to the highest bidder, frequently for an insignifi- 
eant amount, whieh as an item of revenue ean be no important object to 
the Turkish Government, served only as a source of gain to the private 
purchaser, whose vexatious measures to render his privilege as profitable 
48 possible, create infinite inconvenience and prejudice to trade, 

It was in consideration of the abolition by the Porte, of all previously 
existing monopolies and other restrictions and indirect exactions, that 
the high duties established in the Convention of 1838 were conceded, 
Though professedly not intended to apply to foreign trade, all such res- 
trictions and exactions eventually fall upon, or indirect! y affect European 
commerent interests; and | humbly submit, that arrangements with the 
Porte, by which their definite removal might be secured, would prove 
highly advantageous to British trade with 'Turkey, 

In compliance with the concluding paragraph of your Excelleney's 
instructions, I transmit herewith a packet for the Foreign Department, 
containing copies of the present despatch and its inclosure. 

I have, &e., 
(Signed) HY. SUTTER.

Inclosure 2 in No, 15. 
Mr. Consul Suter to Sir Stratford Canning. 


Sir, Kuissariah, July 1, 1845. 

THAVE the honour to transmit herewith an extract of a despatch 
and a copy of its ‘nelogure, which [ have been requested to forward for 
your Excellency’s consideration, regarding the duties on the principal 
exports from Tarsous. 

The observations offered by Mr. Clapperton may in a great measure 
also apply to the exports of this country, and occasion has been offered 
me to remark that merchants trading from Anatolia complain of the 
heaviness with which the present duties bear upon many of its produe- 
tions, owing to the tariff rates'on them of the Convention of 1835 having 
been fixed without a due knowledge and estimate of their real value. j 

L cannot, therefore, but concur in the representation submitted by Mr. 
Clapperton, that a revision and reduction of those rates to a more equitable 
seale were desirable for the greater advantage and encouragement af our 
trade with Turkey. 

I have, &c., 
(Signed) HY. SUTER.

[nclosure 3 in No. 15, 
Mr. Vice-Consul Clapperton to Mr. Consul Suter. 


(Extract.) Tarsous, May 29, 1845. 

AS the Commercial Convention of 1838 between Great Britain and 
the Porte will expire on the dyth Mareh next, and various products of 
this and the adjacent provinces are affected by grievous export duties, 
beyond the basis of the Convention of 12 per cent., | have requested a 
meeting of the principal merchants at Tarsous of all denominations, invtt- 
ing them to give me a price-current of the various articles of export from 
the ports of ‘Tarsous, hased on their average value during the last four 
years, in order to enable my ascertaining and submitting tothe Earl of 
Aberdeen and Her Majesty's Ambassador at Constantinople, how much 
the commerce of this province in particular has been aggrieved by the 
Convention. | 

Under present circumstances this place is precluded the means of 
trading advantageously with England, and of vecel ving her manufactures 
by the high Tarif, and is unable to compete with other provinces of the 
empire, and with other countries, where on articles similar to those 
exported hence, though of double and treble the value, only the same 
amount of duties is exacted, Te ans Ah tela eg eae va 

I beg reference to the accompanying prices current signed by the 
merchants, which is of course confined to the staple productions of these 
reductions, ‘To the prices of the article | have appended the duties pay- 
able on them according to the Tariff, and by the calculated per centage 
of these. on the value of each, it will be seen how far some of them are 
taxed above the duty of 12 per cent. on exports upon which the Conven- 
tion was founded, 

It is worthy of notice that this province seems to be the only one in 
which the customers have not consented to make bonifications to the mer- 
chants from the high rates of the Tariff. In Aleppo, Beyrout, Cyprus, 
and other places, agreements ave been entered into to that effect, the 
hasis of which is as near as possible to the 12 per cent, 

The principal exports henee are cotton, sheep’s wool, wheat, barley, 
sesume seed, linseed, madder roots; besides whieh, wax, old copper, yellow 
berries, goats: wool, Indian corn, lentils, butter, planks, &e , are likewise 
exported, but only in ‘neonsiderable quantities.

luternal duties, 


50 


that in every town in the East, from the Bosphorus to the Himmalaya, 
there have been from time immemorial a variety of local monopolies which 
form the town dues, and which are in fact equivalent to the excise duties 
and taxes on luxuries of European cities. The chief monopolies at Bagdad 
may be classed as follows :—supply of oil, manufacture of soap, trade in 
bitumen, dyeing longeloths, printing ditto, tanning, cleaning raw cotton, 
refining spirits, storing grain, &e., &e. Now the question is, how far 
British merchants can interfere with these municipal regulations. I have 
always asserted the privilege of our merchants to deal freely in all species 
of native produce, importing oil, soap, bitumen, alkali, anil other articles 
which are prohibited to the native traders, but I have felt that in so doing 
we are straining our privileges to the utmost, and in fact that by interfer- 
ing with these monopolies we are seriously injuring the Government, and 
obtaining an undue advantage over the subjects of the Porte. ‘The 
question has been put to meas to whether a British merchant can dye his 
own cloths at Bagdad (having paid the import duty on the cloths and the 
indigo) and then dispose of them without being subjected to any local 
tax, and I have answered in the affirmative; but | must say that | think 
the Porte may complain with justice in this respect of the latitude of 
interpretation allowed to the second clause of our Treaty. 
The above remarks refer to a trade in articles which are positively 
rohibited to the subjects of the Porte, and on which accordingly there 
is no duty that can be made applicable to British merchants, but there is 
still remaining a question of equal embarrassment as to the maximum of 
liability to which a British merchant is subject in dealing in those other 
articles of native produce intended for internal consumption, a trade in 
which is permitted to the subjects of the Porte generally, but which is at 
the same time accompanied by a very heavy if not an oppressive taxation, 
It would be inconvenient to enumerate all the taxes which come under 
this head. ‘They consist of transit duties levied under an hundred dif- 
ferent names, by land and by water, tolls at ferries, bridges, or even at 
the passage of rivers where ferries or bridges have once existed, duties on 
carriage, monopoly of forming rafts for the conveyance of produce, and 
a whole host of minor fees levied at the custom-house, at the gates and in 
the market-places of Bagdad. ‘They compose in fact that formidable 
array of interior taxation, which in reference to produce intended for 
foreign markets was commuted by the Treaty of Balti Liman for a fixed 
duty of 9 per cent. leviable at the port of shipment, but which still con- 
tines to apply to all articles of produce (sheep, grain, tobacco, dates, 
fruit, salt, firewood, vegetables, &c.,&c., &e.) required for home consump 
tion®. In many cases these duties, as they are exacted from subjects of 
the Porte, amount to as much as 20 and sometimes even to 30 Bee cent. 
Now British merchants, although by the srd Article of the ‘Treaty of 
Balti Liman they can only claim equality with the most favoured class of 
Turkish subjects, pretend that that equality has a legal limit. “They 
point to the Turkish Tariff, published under authority and embodying 
the finance regulations of the empire, where the aggregate charge on 
agricultural produce is fixed at 12 per cent.; and they show, moreover, that 
we appear to have had that limitation in view in adopting the same rate 
for the aggregate liability on exports. If a governor then of a distant 
provinee chooses arbitrarily and on his own responsibility to Increase the 
rate from 12 to 20 per cent., they fall back on the Sultan's official gua- 
rantee as their ground of exemption from the local rapacity. 1 have 
hitherto in general succeeded in carrying this point, but never without 
much altercation and delay; and I would recommend accordingly, that in 
the revision of the Treaty there should be a distinet clause, defining in 
how fara British merchant engaged in the interior trathe of Turkey ean 
interfere with the old-established and still-existing monopolies, and also to 
what agerezate amount of taxation he may be Hable, in the purchase, 
transmission, and sale of agricultural produce intended for internal con- 
sumption. 
® J see in lis Highness the Soltan’s Hatti-Shoriff dated June 20, 1846, there is a formal 
engagement to anwal the hticsab duties from next March, ‘This will be a boow of the most vital 
importance to the poorer elisses of His Higliness’ subjects, and will unquestionibly greatly tend to 
the encouragement and extension of agriculture. 


51 


Imports. 


_ The attention of the Government appears in the Treaty of Balti 
Liman to have been chiefly drawn to the importation of British goods into 
Turkey, and certainly nothing can be more favourable than the conditions 
applying to that branch of trade. 

| have never been able however to assure myself of the 


al 


meaning Of Meaning of the Int 


+ Covoran ae. r in * oi Aa 5 
the First Additional Article, and 1 have sustained much inconvenience in. Additional Article. 


consequence, 

The English draft indeed and the Turkish translation appear to be 
af variance on a very important question contained in this article: as | 
understand the Treaty, there are two distinct classes of foods, which are 
entitled to the privilege of import at 3 percent, Firstly, British or colonial 
gooils in the hands of Englishmen, foreigners or Turkish subjects: and 
secondly. all merchandize (that is, foreign as well as British roots) being 
the property of British subjects and imported in British vessels, and 
further consider the Government in f ‘aming the Article in question to have 
aimed at combining a general protection of British goods and consequent 
extension of British commerce, with the acquisition of a special privilege 
for Mnelish merchants, in enabling them to import foreign goods on the 
same terms as those of our own country. The Turkish functionaries, 
however, in this quarter insist on reading the Article in question, as if the 
expression “embarked in British vessels and being the property of British 
subjects,” referred to the two preceding clauses of the sentence, that is. to 
British goods as weil as to “all merehandive;” and they fortify their 
argument by protesting against the possibility of the right having been 
concede: to a British agent to interpose his authority between the Purkish 
Government and Turkish subjects, merely because the latter parties have 
thought fit to import produce from India, ‘The question at the same time 
is one of considerable importance in regard to the trade with India. and 
merits, | think, some attention. Your Excellency will have seen from my 
report on the trade of Bussora, that goods the growth, produce and 
manulacture of a British dependency (namely our colonial possessions in 
India), are annually brought into that port to the value of nearly 100,0002. 
Searcely a tenth part however of this trade is in the British subjects. 
Phe main importers are Arab and Jew merchants, subjects of the Porte 
established at Bagdad; and the rate of duty which they have hitherto 
paid varies according to the different articles, from 7 to about 12 per cent. 
About 20,000/, worth of the import is also subsequently transmitted into 
Persia by the same parties who commission the goods from India, and on 
this stock, which should as I believe be subject to no more than the 
original liability of 3 per cent. on importation, a further tax is levied on 
quitting Bagdad, British merchants are of course exempt from similar 
charges, and they are thus enabled to undersell their native competitors, 
but T doubt if the individual benefit necruing to these parties can be held 
to compensate for the general check which our Indian trade sustains by 
the distinction. ‘The only difficult question appears to be as to who may 
be the proper party to interfere in favour of the importers. 'The Turks 
will certainly resist a claim that a British agent shall protect Turkish 
subjects, merely because the latter parties engage in the Indian trade, and 
yet in ho other way can the provisions forthe unimpeded admission of our 
goods he duly realized, | 


F ne Ura ii 14° | ine : 7 4 1 
Your Excellency will pereeive that Messrs. Hector and Co. have River dues 


again brought forward the question of the river dues, maintaining: that 
the said dues constitute a tax upon British imports, in excess of the 2 per 
cent commuitation for interior duties. As the Turks, however have Woe 
drawn a distinetion between British and Native bottoms, admitting the 
former to ascend the river of Mesopotamia with a earga from India 
without being: subject to any extra payment, while they have determined
