and pareel of the soil of the country, and Englishmen being prohibited 
from holding landed or household property in the Turkish Empire. Itmay 
be satisfactory however to your Excellency to learn that | have resisted 
payment of the Qalibiyeh duty, which forms the heaviest item ol the river 
dues, on a ground which [have only lately discovered, but Ww nich appears 
under present circumstances to be unexceptionable. Many years ago 
the Ottoman Government desirous of checking the disposition manifested 
by its subjects to attach themselves to the European Consulates, granted 
special firmans to some of the most respectable Turkish merchants, 
alacing them on an equality in every respect with foreign traders, end 
specifying the local privileges which they would thus enjoy. : One ae 
privileges so conferred at Bagdad was exemption f om the Qa bie 
duty, and I have accordingly stated that as long as these , pmneas Fonate 
in force at Bagdad, our merchants can claim the benefit ol the incul- 
gencies which they confer, according to the Third Article of the Treaty, 
which entitles us to the same privileges as the most favoured class of 
Turkish subjects. Bhontd the firmans be cancelled, the point however 
be I fear conceded. 
a ee only remains that | should notice in Pant to the Treaty, the 
question of British goods imported into Bagdad for transmission to 
other countrics. Ifa British merchant enter goods at the ports of Syria 
or at Bussora for transmission to Persia, he merely pays of COUTSE: the 
import duty of 3 per cent.; but it is rarely that he CON thus beforehand 
determine the amount and description of articles which will not he 
required in the Turkish market, {t is in fact merely the merchandize 
which does not command a ready sale at Bagdad, that he finds it 
advisable to export, and he is thus obliged to pay the extra 2 per cent, on 
his whole investment. Messrs, Hector and Co, have suggested that the 
system of drawbacks should be introduced as in Murope ; but in the 
resent constitution of the machine of Government in the Turkish Km pire, 
[ cannot consider such an arrangement to be at all practicable. The 
farmer, for instance, of the Bagdad customs would be extremely loth 
to disburse the 2 per cent. levied by his competitor at Bussora, and in 
this case the two custom-houses are under the same Government, How 
then could it be expected that he should make a singular disbursement 
on account of sums realised at Beyrout, Alexandretta, or Samson, places 
over the financial concerns of which the Bagdad Government cannot 
exercise any control whatever? If any alteration should be attempted 
(and the obseure and almost irreconcileable provisions of the fh irst and 
Third of the Additional Articles certainly require emendation in this 
respect) | would venture to recommend that the 3 per cent. should be 
alone levied at the place of importation, and that the goods should be 
placed in bond at the interior custom-house where the importer may 
reside, the additional 2 per cent. being: levied at that interior custom- 
house on such portion of the goods as might be cleared out for sale on 
the spot, and the remainder being transmitted direct from the bonded 
lace of deposit, free of any further charge to a foreign country. At 
agdad the difficulty is chiefly felt in regard to goods imported from 
India through Bussora for the consumption af W estern Persia; and ns 
I have before observed, the parties engaged in this trade being subjects 
of the Porte, the Turkish authorities, aceording to their interpret ation of 
the first sentence of the First Additional Article, will not admit of my 
interference, ; ee (1M AOL ie

Another question regards the adjudication of mercantile 
Fraudulent transactions are of the most common occurrence a 
on the part of the native traders in their dealings with 
merchants, and in all cases the points at issue are referre 
to the Municipal Council. Now this council is com 
part of fanatical priests, who invariably support ‘the Mahomedan against 
the Christian, and it thus happens that in no one single instance have I 
obtained for my clients what I consider to be even-handed justice, I 
would propose, therefore, as the Capitulations are deficient in any sTOvVI- 
sions for the adjustment of suits in which Englishinen are complainants 
in the provinces, we should be in such cases under authority privile, 
to be judged by the Governor, who, being thus personally responsible 
would for his own credit take care to give an equitable decision. If 
indeed we could only get the principle acknowledged of our right to be 
judged in equity, instead of according to the (in many respects absurd) 
code of Mahomedan law, we should obtain, I think, a guarantee of no 
small advantage to the security and extension of our commerce. 

The 2 per cent fee on realization, on which 
Excellency in a former letter, has certainly also, although sanctioned by 
the Seventy-third Article of the Capitulations, grown into a gross abuse at 
Bagdad. No debtor will now pay a British merchant regularly or 
willingly, knowing that the latter party will give time rather than be 
saddled with the additional expense of employing’ a Government cavass 
In the inelosure No. 2 to your eycellencys despatch No, 3 of the 25th of 
March last, it is stated that at Constantinople the Turkish authority is 


only appealed to recover debts for British creditors when other measures 
have proved ineffectual. What those other measures may be, however, I 


am unable to devine, At Bagdad certainly there is no middle course 
available between demanding payment of the debtor and complaining 
through the Consulate to the Government, The capitulation in question 
may have been suited to our relations with Turkey 200 years ago, but | 
think your Excellency will admit that it is uninst in principle, and that it 
is hardly becoming’ our present influential position in the Ottoman

compelled to appeal to authority for the realization of his dues.

oo 


say one-third or one-half of the imports are re-exported into Persia, but 
under the disadvantage of having paid the full amount of the two duties, 
i. e. the 3 per cent and 2 per cent. in Syria, in consequence of their having 
changed hands; it would therefore be of a decided advantage to the 
import of British manufactures here, if in revising the Treaty a clause 
could be added, stating that the 2 per cent. should only be paid when the 
goods are sold for retail, and mot when transmitted or sold for transmis- 
sion into the interior. If the goods remained in the hands of the importer 
the matter would be simple enough; but this is not the case, they pass 
through several hands in the course of transit; and I would therefore sub- 
mit, in transmitting goods from Syria to Bagiad for instance under the 
@ per cent, that a guarantee should be taken from the parties clearing out 
the goods, providing for the production of a certilicate within a certain 
time of the goods having been duly entered at the custom-house for which 

they were cleared out. ‘The same remarks will also apply to the trans. 

mission of goods from Bussora, The goods having arya at Bagilad, I 

would suggest that in clearing them out, if they are not exported at once 

into Persia (which will very seldom happen as all Orientals prefer exami- 

ning the goods’, the party concerned should pay the 2 per cent, duty or 

be responsible for it, aud report such roads as he may afterwards export 

or sell for exportation, a claim the drawback; by this menns, the 

custom-house here would receive 2 per cent. on the goods consumed in the 

place and lose nothing on the export to Persia. 

Srdly. Among the principal articles of British manufactures consumed 
in Bagdad and its vicinity, are white 'Tanjibs and bleached longcloths, 
(which are printed by the hand into a peeuiar kind of Arab handkerchief 
which we cannot imitate ii Mngland,) these articles are subjeeu by 
adyeing monopoly to about S77 per cent. on the value of the article, (as 

yer annexed Memorandum marked No. 2.) notwithstanding the dye itself 
eing entircly British produce, and haying paid the duty on importation. 
If this monopoly, eich | hald to be an indireet taxation on this particular 
class of manufactures, were removed, the consumption would at least be 
double, and I would strongly recommend the abolition of it being 
insisted upon. 

4thly, Another serious drawback to Dvitish trade here is the amount 
of duty levied upon loaded boats on their passage from Bussora to Bagdad, 
and vice versa, Which amounts on the passage up, to4,1554 plastres of Con- 
stantinople, and on the passage down to 11364 (as per annexed Memorandum 
No. 3.) These duties are sanctioned and authorized by the Local Govern- 
ment, part of the amount being actually paid to the Government autho- 
rities at Bagdad and Bussora. They are levied directly on the cargoes, 
and not upon the boats, as empty boats pass free. The Arab chiefs, 
also, on the bank of the river, who make the exactions, are under the 
control of the Turkish Government, and pay regularly a large tribute 
inmoney, part of which is thus obtained; so that the duties aie in 
the above manner are actually an increase by the 'lurkish Government on 
those sanctioned by the Treaty. In 1842 we laid this case before Colonel 
Taylor, then Her Majesty's Consul at Bagdad, aud in February 1843, 
before His Majesty’s Ambassador at Constantinople, in the hope of 
recovering a large sum of money exacted from us ona large quantity of 
British manufactures. At the same time we stated that the charges in 
question were only partially levied, and that trading-vessels of persons 
connected with the Government (as Abdul Kader Agha, the late Gum- 
rukehee of Bagdad, for instance) paid nothing, And again, we think it 
proper to remark that in June 1546, a large trading-boat belonging to 
some person connected with the present Government came up from Bus- 
sora, laden with a cargo of merchandixe, the nokada of which was fur- 
nished with letters from the Government to the authorities on the river, 
which exempted her entirely from the payment of any of these duties, 


while other boats in company with her were obliged to pay. If any 


doubt, therefore, remained on the subject, this would prove that the duties 
in question are taken with the concurrence and sanction of the Govern- 
I beg to mention a circumstance that has just occurred, in order 


Pp 


ment 


33 


 * 


56 


to show how aggravating these taxes are. We are now sending down a 
boat of our own to Bussora, and at the same time havea few bales 
of wool which we also wish to transmit; but as the quantity is very 
small, and as the circumstance of the boat having one single bale on 
board renders her liable to the whole duties, we have preferred sending 
her down empty and paying a heavy extra freight for the conveyance of 
our wool to Bussora by another boat, which ts laying on for a full cargo, 
in order to enable her to meet the taxes. I may add, that the boats 
trading between this and Bussora, although not bond fide British-built, 
have still some claim to consideration, for every plank and nail contained 
in them, as well as all their rigging and materials, are imported from 
India, and have consequently paid the import duties before they are put 
together. ae | 

Sthly. The raft monopoly at Moossul is another tax on British goods 
in their transit from Syria to Bagdad, At present that monopoly charges 
for the conveyance of goods from Moossul to Bagcne 100 Constantinople 
piastres per cantar of 600 Ibs., two-thirds of which go to the farmers:of 
the monopoly and one-third to the people who really construct the rafts 
and convey the goods to Bagdad; thus making a direct tax of 66% pias- 
tres on every cantar of goods, which on an average amounts to about 
1 per cent. on the value of the goods. | Be 

Gthly. There are two other questions deeply affecting British trade 
at this place, which I shall embrace in the present remarks, notwithstandin 
T had the honour of addressing you on the same annie under date 20t 
January last, viz. the tax of 2 per cent. upon the entire sum which 
British merchants are compelled to pay to Government officers, who are 
employed in collecting their debts. Although Lam aware that this levy 
is authorized by the 73rd Article of the Capitnlations, we may perhaps be 
allowed to remark upon the great inconvenience and loss to which we are 
subjected by it. It acts as an inducement to our debtors to defer pay- 
ment of their debts, and to contract their payments when called upon by 


our agents, as in both instances the inconvenience and loss fall upon the 


merchant alone. These facts have become so notorious that the merchant 
is now compelled to have recourse to Government officers for the recovery 
of at least three-fourths of his debts. It would seem but reasonable that 


as it is not customary to receive interest on debts withheld, the debtors 


who wilfully persist in deferring payment of their just liabilities should 
be subjected to the expenses arising from their refusal and delay. — 
The other circumstance which [ would beg to submit is, that in cases 


of natives of Turkey becoming insolvent and absconding, if they have — 


landed property, it appears from the present law of Turkey, such property 
cannot be sequestrated and sold for the benefit of their creditors durin 
their absence. This is a circumstance which frequently occurs, an 
subjects the merchant to great inconvenience and loss; and it is an 
inducement for insolvent debtors to abscond, knowing that their absence 
is a protection to their property. I would therefore suggest that in such 
cases some provision be made to enable the local authorities to exercise 
the right of sequestering and disposing of absentee debtors’ property for 
the benefit of those concerned. 

Trusting that, should you consider these hasty remarks worthy of 
consideration, you will lay them before Her Majesty’s Ambassador at the 
Porte, 


We have, &c., _ 
(Signed) ALEXANDER HECTOR & Co.

Inclosure 4 in No. 17. 


Messrs. Lynch and Co. to Major Rawlinson. 


Bagdad, August 7, 1846. 
WE have the honour to acknow- 
ledge receipt of your letter of the 
9th ultimo, under cover, with an 
extract of a despatch dated the Sth 
of June last, aaneane. to you by his 
Excellency the British Ambassador 
at Constantinople, regarding the 
bearing of the existing ‘Tariff on our 
commercial interests here, and re- 
quiring any observations which our 
trade may have practically sug- 
reated, ui 
2. In reply to which, and with 
reference to the Tariff, we should 
wish to remark that we always 
found that the two duties therein 
fixed on articles of export, being the 
roduce of Bagdad and its vicinity, 
ar exceeded 12 per cent. ad valorem, 
when they come to be exported at 
Bagdad, for instance, alls, madder 
and hides. ‘The higher value of 
these articles in the purts of the 
Mediterranean and Black Sea in- 
duced, perhaps, those gentlemen 
who framed the Tarill to calculate 
the article at a high valuation, for 
we have always, alter reference to 
the Tariff, and comparison of the 
price at Bagdad with their fixed 
duties, been led, when possible, 
to abandon the Tariff and have 
recourse to the 12 Bee cent. ad 
valorem, areys attended with dif- 
ficulty and delay, 


It will be seen that Messrs Lynch 
and Co, admit their being allowed 
the local privilege of abandoning 
the fixed rates of the Tariff, in favour 
of an ad valorem liability, but state 
that the arrangement is attended 
with difficulty and delay. My own 
opinion is, that the "Tariff, both in 
reference to exports and imports, 
has been in the Bagdad Pashalie up 
to the present time almost inopera- 
tive. 


a9 


Messrs, Lynch and Co. appear to 
refer to the preamble of the Russian 
Tariff of 1844, in which there is no 
allusion toa liability to interiorduties 
or to a fixed commutation for such 
duties, and in virtue of which Arme- 
nian traders certainly, at this place, 
furnished with Russian passports, 
have succeeded not unfrequently in 
importing goods at an advantage of 
2 per cent. over British merchants. 

It has always appeared to me, 
however, that the interior duties for 
which the French and English 
accepted a. fixed liability, and which 
were then generally abolished on 
European imports, might be revived 
with a special application to Russia, 
so long as that Government declined 
to enter into a convention similar to 
thatof Balti Liman, and that British 
merchants would, undersuch cireun- 
stances, have the advantage of their 
Russian competitars. It is to be 
presumed that the question of in- 
terior duty has been definitively 
settled in the new Russian Treaty, 
and whatever privileges may have 
beenconeeded by that Treaty to the 
Government in question ‘will of 
course accordingly become our 
right by the First Article of the 
Convention of Balti Liman, 


The question of the boat duty has 
already been amply stated and con- 
sidered. It is declared to be a lia- 
lility appertaining to the interior 
traflic of the country, and is Sup- 
posed to be incurred by British mer- 
chants for the privilege of navigra- 
ting interior ‘Turkish waters in 
Turkish bottoms. The merchants 
would be relieved from it by bring- 
ing’ their goods direct to Bagdad in 
British registered vessels, or by 
having them conveyed overland 
from Bussora, Compensation for 
plunder would fall heavily on the 
Government of an exposed province 
like that of Bagdad, where the roads 
are infested in every direction b 
wandering Arab and Kurdish free- 
booters, who are not only independ- 
ent af controul but who eould hardly 
be brought under controul by any 
means within the compass of the 
Turkish Government as it is at pre- 
sent constituted and administered. 


3. The progressive cheapness of 
English goods has caused the im- 
port duty fixed by the Tariff to 
amount to 4 per cent. on its present 
value instead of 3 per cent.—thig 
applies to goods of every deseri 
tion ; and the chances of these goods 
becoming still much cheaper might 
induce a revision of the present 
Tariff on this occasion, when we 
hope the interior duty of 2 per cent, 
(no longer paid by our Russian 
competitors) will be removed, 


4. It would be greatly to the 
interest and extension of our trade 
did our Government, in considera. 
tion of our peculiar position here, 
conclude arrangements with the 
Sublime Porte, relative to the secu- 
rity from exactions or plunder of 
our goods in transit, to be in every 
possible instance accounted for to 
the British merchant by the local 
Government: we do here particu. 
larly allude to those exactions levied 
on our boats laden with English or 
Indian goods (empty boats and 
those belonging to’ Goyernment 
being alone clear) by the Arabs 
under Government ‘sanction or 
otherwise, To exempt us on the 
‘lees while in transit, from paying 
those exactions would be exposing 
the goods to the rage of these free. 
booters. It would be wise, there- 
fore, in this respect to deal with 
Government alone, who could repay 
themselves in their annual miri 
taken from these people, and we 
should suggest here the same rule 
to hold good with respect to plun- 
dered property, as these heav y sums 


ghd as duty to the Arabs, and 


35 


Se 


60 : 


losses from plunder, greatly retard 
and seriously effect the extension of 
British trade in this pashalic, 


5. The ides seemingly se- 
cured by Treaty of transporting 
Turkish produce from place to place 
without paying district tolls or 
bddge, which in this pashalic 
amount generally to a sum rang- 
ine from 12 to 20 per cent, on its 
value, making the same equal to a 
monopoly, is so annulled by the 
3rd Article of the Treaty placing 
us in competition with the native, 
who to trade generally monopolizes 
the revenue of the particular articles

however advantageous it might be 
to the merchants to restore them in

Inclosure in No. 18, 


Mr. Vice-Consul Rassam to Sir Stratford Canning. 
Sir, Moossul, July 10, 1846. 
I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Excellency’s 
despatch marked cireular, and dated the sth ultimo, directine me ‘to 
furnish statements in detail respecting the several points in which the 
Tariff of 1539 may require amendment, with such suggestions for im prove- 
ment as I might be able to obtain from British merchants within my 
district, 

On the bearing of the Tariff'as respects imports no remark offers to 
me. ‘This branch of our trade, once of considerable extent, | am sorry to 
inform your Excellency has rapidly fallen off within the last few years 
partly owing to the Swiss manufactures having in some degree supp anted 
the use of our printed cottons, but chiefly to the impoverished state of this 
rovinee; and the only means now left to restore the import trade to its 
ormer position is to encourage that in exports, 

Moossul possesses only three articles of export for the exterior, viz.: 
Relen a sheep's wool, and sesame seed. The first have for many years 
aeen sent by the native dealers in large quantities to Aleppo, whence the 
find their way to Europe. The second has come into notice during the 
last two years, at all events till then the quantity exported was very incon- 
considerable, whereas this year nearly the whole growth has been pur- 
chased for Europe, For the third there yet remains a fair trial to be 
made, There are other descriptions of produce grown here, such as 
grain, cotton, and tobacco, but these are either used on the spot or are 
consumed within the neighbouring pashalics. 

The export trade influences that in imports in this manner, As I 
have already frequently exposed to your Excellency, this province has been 
nearly exhausted of specie by a succession of rapacious governors, who 
have not left the peo He the means to purchase our manufactures, however 


much they may need them, Therefore our only plan is to take produce 
instead of coin in payment. But the present ealiatigd fixed in lieu of the 


36 


=e 5 


62 


9 and 3 per cent. ad valorem on articles of export is much overrated, even 
in some cases on the Constantinople prices, and would form a creat draw- 
back to the extension of the trade which it ought to be as much the desire 
of the Porte to encourage as it can be ours, were it not that by a special 
firman obtained by Injeéh Mahomed Pasha the duties that ought to be 
paid at the sea-ports are allowed to be received by this custom-house, 
which always calculates them at 12 per cent. on the actual price of the 
day, than which nothing can be more fair, 

By the Tarilf the duty fixed on gall-nuts is 3,888 aspers for the 9 per 
cent., and 1296 for the 3 per cent., equal together to 431 piastres per kin- 
tal, which amounts to nearly 16 per cent. on 6 piastres per oke, a good 
price for the better qualities at Constantinople, and on 4 piastres per oke, 
what they would cost at Iskanderoon, isequal to about 204 per cent.; and 
on the value ‘of about 3 piastres per oke to 29 per cent. 

On sheep's wool the Tariffis rated at 2,214 and 738 aspers, or 24 
piastres per kintal, equal to about 12 per cent. on 200 piastres, a fair price 
at Constantinople, which is what it also comes to at Alexandretta; and 
on the first cost here of about 3 piastres per oke to about 18 per cent. 

Sesame is rated at 302 and 100 aspers per kilo of 20 okes, or of 
piastres 315, Ido not know what is the value of this seed at Constanti- 
nople. Itcan be sent to Alexandretta at a cost of 2 piastres per oke, 
making the duty equal to about 8} per cent.; but on the value here of 
about 31 paras, equals nearly 22 per cent. on first cost, 

It would seem at all events thir that the internal duty of 9 per cent. 
should be calculated on the prices of the places of growth, and not as is 
now the case, on those of the sea-ports, where the price of poor goods has 
been so greatly enhanced by an expensive land carriage, as in some cases 
to have more than doubled. 

One great advantage would be entirely to abolish the vexatious sys- 
tem of “ Badjs” and all similar transit dues to be found on the Tigris’ at 
Tikrit and at various places on the caravan roads between Bagdad, Moos- 
sul, and Aleppo. which being generally arbitrary and consequently 
searcely ever fixed, can merely be calculated beforehand, even when their 
existence is known. Although it is contrary to treaty, there is a con- 
stant endeavour to oblige payment of these “ Badjs” éven on our mann- 
factures which have paid the 3 and the 2 per cent. at the sea-ports, and 
also on Turkish produce purchased for exportation, which of course gives 
rise to numerous complaints and references in the case of European 
merchants, and in that of the natives they have quietly to submit. It 
would seem much more simple to fix regular duties on goods intended for 
internal trade, ney ? per cent, on entry into a chief town, and 3 per cent. 
on departure, and after receiving a regular teskéré the goods to be free 
from all further imposts, 

Grain, by the Mahomedan law, exeept when leaving Turkey, is 
exempt from all dues beyond the “ashur” or tithe, but nevertheless a duty 
of 12 per cent. is exacted on its removal from one pashalic to another, 
and sometimes also * Badj.” Now were it again to he free, the famines 
which occasionally occur in these countries would not be felt so severely 
as they now are, for there would be a greater inducement to transport 
eorn from districts where it abounded, to others where it was scarce, and 
this facility might even perhaps stimulate a more extensive cultivation, 
for which there is so much room. A starving district: pays sufficiently 
for imported grain in carriage alone. Wheat was purchased here this 
year at 70 to 50 piastres the toghar, and went to Aleppo at a carriage of 
160 piastres. :

My Lord, Broumana, September 8, 1846. 


I HAVE the honour to inclose to your Lordship a copy of my des- 


patch No. 2 to Her Majesty’s Minister Plenipotentiary at the Porte, in 
reply to his Excellency Sir Stratford Canning’s circular of the 30th May 
last. 


I venture to observe that it is a general opinion amongst the best 


informed persons, that the export duty of 12 per cent. is too high, and 
acts prejudicially, not only on the Turkish ‘Treasury, but on the British 
mercantile interest. 


These persons think that British capital would be applied to the 
improvement of other Syrian products besides silk, were the duty lowered 


so as to give them a remunerating price. 


Of course if the export of products were increased the Turkish 
revenue would be proportionately increased, ae 

1 know of nothing more likely to encourage the extension of British 
trade to the south of Syria than the reduction of the export duties. 

There is a peculiarly fine quality of oil produced in the neighbourhood 
of Acre which would form an important branch of commerce. 

With respect to the statement of Mr. Consul Finn relative to the 
state of commerce in the south of Syria, | have the honour to say that 
there have never been any more British commercial houses than the one 
specified by Mr. Finn; therefore there has been no falling off in that 
respect. + ‘ sage 

The only place where British trade has ever existed to any extent 
is Beyrout, Alexandretta and Aleppo. wn 

The annual returns show that in these places British trade has been 
gradually increasing since 1840, 

The Vice-Consul of Sidon is in mistake as to the monopoly of coffee. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) HUGH ROSE. 


Inclosure 1 in No. 19. 


Lieutenant-Colonel Rose to the Hon. H, Wellesley. 


Sir, Broumana, August 23, 1846. 

IN compliance with the instructions contained in his Excellency Sir 
Stratford Canning's circular of the 30th of May last, Ihave the honour 
to inclose to you a copy of a report from Mr, Consul Moore, inclosing five 
communications from the British merchants of Beyrout, dol oea a points 
in which the Tariff of 1838 in their opinion requires amendment. 

My report would have been transmitted at an earlier period, but I 
waited for the arrival of the reports from the different Consulates. That 
from Jerusalem has not yet reached me. The native Viee-Consul at Tyre 
by the direction of Mr, Consul Finn has sent me his report; but as it 
merely relates to internal trade I do not forward it. 

| do not inelose the reports of Her Majesty’s Consuls at Aleppo and 
Damascus, because those gentlemen inform me that they have transmitted 
them directly to Her Majesty's Embassy, | 

The several reports from Syria appear to me to be generally so well 
drawn up and so grounded on fact, that little is left for me to say in the 
way of observation or information. 


37 


64 


I think, however, that it may be of use to present to you, In a con- 
densed form, the substance of the opinions of the British Consuls and 
merchants in Syria, and then to offer a few observations relative to them 
and the amendment of the Tariff, 

1. Mr. Consul Werry points out the inconvenience resulting to com- 
merce from an infraction of the 1Vth Article of the Convention of 1838, 
which states that the 9 and 3 per cent. on exports shall be paid at the 
place of shipment and on exportation. Lae 

Mr. Werry also states the inconvenience caused by the desire of the 
farmers of custom-houses at different sea-ports to gain for themselves the 
import duties of 3.and 2 per cent., or part of them, and of that of the 
merchants to save them. blue 

Mr. Werry finally shows that a duty called “ Badj,” which presses 
on British ecommerce, is levied indirectly on goods, on the plea of protec- 
tion and improved state of roads, neither of which however exist. 

The merchants of Aleppo desire a revisal of the Tariff, which on 
account of the change in the Turkish currency and other reasons which 
they state, considerably exceed the ad valorem rates established by the 
Convention of 1858. aw) in 

2, Mr. Consul Moore opines also for a revision of the Tariff rates. 

Mr, Moore points out the difference of interests which he thinks 
seems to induce the British merchants who import to prefer upon the 
whole the establishment of an ad valorem duty, and the exporters to oppose 
it in the same degree. 

Mr. Moore thinks also that the high rate of 12 per cent. export duty 
is prejudicial to the British manufacturer and exporter, the revenue, and 
the producer, _ 

The British merchants of Beyrout are anxious for a revisal of the 
rates of the present ‘Tariff, for the same reasons as those stated by the 

merchants, 
iii above is the substance of the opinions of the British Consuls and 
merchants resident in those parts of Syria in which there is the most con- 
siderable British commercial interest. I have not alluded to any observa- 
tion based on the supposition that Russians still trade with advantage over 
British merchants, inasmuch as the new Russian Treaty has caused those 
advantages to disappear. J ‘* 

I now have the honour to offer a few observations on the opinions 
transmitted from Syria, and to make one or two suggestions as to amend- 
ments in the Tariff and as to British trade in this country. 

The merchants of Damascus are in error, | conceive, firstly, in sup- 
nosing that there is any doubt as to where the duty of 9 per cent. should 
Sara inasmuch as the [Vth Article of the Convention of 1536 specilies 
that it is to be paid on its entry to the place of shipment; and secondly as 
regards the advantage of paying the duty at the place of produce, because 
by the Tariff the duty is reduced to a fixed rate in aspers, which rate 
would be the same, whether paid at the place of produce, or on entry at 
the port of shipment. Js 

As regards imports I think that the opinions are well grounded that 
the Tariff should be amended, so that the rates of duty should be regu- 
lated with regard to the change of eurrency and the reduced quality and 
price of goods; in other words, that goods imported for the future should 
only pay in reality the import duties of 3 and 2 per cent., according to 
the wording and spirit of the Convention of 1838, ‘ 

This desideratum might be attained by giving extension to the classi- 
fication of imports of the same kind but of different qualities and colours, 
and allowing the importer to pay in kind if he preferred it, where the 
duty is payable ad valorem. 

With respect to an ad valorem duty on imports it would appear that 
the merchants do not desire its adoption, except on those articles of which 
the classification is very difficult. ' 

In speaking of imports I beg leave to direct your attention to the 
nseful table drawn up by Mr. Black. ant 

As regards exports | feel bound to support the opmion that the duty 
of 12 per cent. on silk is so high as to render the profit of the legitimate 


69 


trader precarious, when he must compete with the silk trader in other 
countries, where silk is not subject to so high an export duty, as well as 
with the advantages enjoyed by the contraband trader, 

Moreover, it is clear that so high a duty as 12 per cent, on so valu- 
able and portable an article as silk, in a country like the Lebanon, moun- 
tainous to the very shore, deprives the customs of a considerable share of 
duty by giving scope to the contraband trader, more especially as the 
maintenance of a preventive service is expensive in proportion as it is 
effective. 

On the other hand, I must observe that the silk reeled and exported 
by British or European merchants does not in reality pay 12 per cent. 
export duty, inasmuch as the 12 per cent. was laid on raw, that is, country 
manufactured silk, which is of much inferior value, as will be shown 
below. 

For example, the Arab uses nine okes of cocoons or thereabouts to 
reel one oke of silk, the European thirteen okes or thereabouts to reel 
the same quantity. 

Therefore the European only pays the same duty on exporting silk 
manufactured from thirteen okes of cocoons, that the Arab pays on that 
manufactured from nine, 

A kilogramme of European-reeled silk fetches from fifty to frances 
at Marseilles, whereas the kilogramme of Arab-reeled silk fetches only 
twenty-eight frances, both being made in the Lebanon from the same 
cocoons. | 

For these reasons the British exporting manufacturer is opposed to 
an ad valorem duty on silk, and I venture to express my entire concurrence 
in his opinion, 

The duty ad valorem on silk would be a tax on the outlay of British 
capital and the industry and skill of our merchants, would in fact prevent 
the continuance of the manufacture and exportation of silk to England, 
which is almost the only return trade of importance for British imports. 

The whole of the silk manufactured in the two British establishments 
in this country is imported into Great Britain, and worked up there, thus 
employing a great number of hands, 

Whilst on the subject of the silk trade | cannot dwell sufficiently on 
the importance of giving all possible encouragement to this new and 
promising branch of trade with Great Britain. 

The Porte did once contemplate, and had even sanctioned to a certain 
extent, a reduction of the 12 per cent. export duty on silk in the Lebanon; 
and one of the chief grounds for this concession was that, as no corn grows 
in the Lebanon, the price of food was proportionately higher there, 

With respect to the inconvenience which has arisen from demands of 
the export duties by different farmers of the custom-houses, a remedy is 
found in a strict adherence to the IInd and [Vth Articles of the Conven- 
tion, the former of which is very stringent. 

As regards the convenience mentioned by Mr, Werry, which results 
from the desire of rival farmers of customs to gain the import duties or a 
mart of them, and of the merchants to save them, this evil arises solely, as 

had the honour to state in 1843, from the system established by the 
Turkish Government of farming the customs of different districts to differ- 
ent parties, who gamble against one another, each party endeavouring’ to 
induce the merchant to pass his goods through his custom-house by offer- 
ing him a reduced rate of duty, 

Ido not: know of any effectual remedy for an evil which owes ita 
existence to the system of Appalto. 

As | am on the subject of imports [ think that it may be apposite to 
observe, that [ have reason to think that it would be an amendment of 
the Convention, were the [Vth Article of the new Russian Treaty of Com- 
meree adopted in lieu of our First Additional Article, which specifies that 
the commutation import duty is to be paid on the sale of goods, or on 
their being sent inte’the interior ; for thus a greater advantage and facility 
would be alforded to the seller, and the purchaser would be relieved from 
the harassing anuoyances to which he ts now subjected by the present 

system of collecting the 2 per cent. duty at the gates, and which in the 


65 


case of small purchases affords an opportanity to. the subordinate collec- 
tors to exact more than the Tariff duty. ' 

But if the [Vth Article of the new Russian Treaty were to be substi- 
tuted for our Ist Additional Article, it should be stipulated further, that in 
case the goods should be shipped from the port of disembarkation for 
another port in the Turkish Empire, the bond for the 2 per cent, commu- 
tation duty should be returned to the importer. } 

It is my duty to state in conclusion, that the Turkish authorities in 
Svria have continued the monopolies of salt and leeches, a circumstance 
which has been communicated to Her Majesty’s Embassy. 

I have, &e., 
(Signed) HUGH ROSE.

Inclosure 2 in No. 1%. 
Mr. Consul Moore to Lieutenant Colonel Kose. 


Sir, Beyrout, July 29, 1846. 

ON the receipt of your circular No. 2, of the 24th ultimo, directing 
me to obtain for your information from the British merchants at this 
place, statements in detail respecting the several points in which the 
Tariff of 1838 may require amendment, with any suggestions for improve- 
ment; I circulated the same amongst the commercial establishments 
here, and have now the honour to melose five separate communications 
addressed to me upon the subject. 

The reasons adduced by the merchants of Beyrout for a revision of 
the Tariffare clear and cogent; any additional observations on my part 
would therefore be superfluous. 

It will be observed by the inclosed statements that some of the com- 
mercial houses here are exclusively engaged in the export trade, and that 
the business of others is almost entirely confined to imports, Their 
observations are accordingly directed to points of the 'Tariffmore imme- 
diately connected with their respective interests. 

If an ad valorem rate of duty would upon the whole seem to be 
preferred oh importers, those engaged in the export trade would I 
apprehend be averse to that scale, Indeed, one of the most considerable 
of the export traders insilk expressed a decided objection toan ad valorem 
duty, alleging as a reason, that as silk reeled according to the European 
system, as itis by the English houses here, bears a much hieher value 
ee that wound by the ordinary process of the country, they would have 
to pay a proportionately higher duty, The Tariff rate is that of raw 
silk. Strictly speaking the reeled silk is manufactured, 

ep i one point however there is perfect unanimity amongst all classes 
of traders here, both British and foreign, and that is in respect to the 
excessive rate of duty upon raw silk, the staple commodity of this part 
of Syria, 

So high a duty as 12 per cent. is not only injurious to the producer, 
increasing as it does so materially the price of Syrian silk in the Kuropean 
market, but also acts prejudicially in respect to the revenue by offering 
a strong inducement to smuggling. 

The English silk establishments urgently represent the injury the 
fair trader receives under a system so favourable as the present is (a high 
rate of duty and an inefficient revenue service) to the unprineipled trader ; 
and | would respectfully direct attention to the observations of Mr. 
Taylor and of Mr, Scott upon this point. . 

As the object of the Turkish Government in levying 50 high a duty 
is not to repress the trade in silk but solely with a view to revenue, that 
object is defeated by the encouragement thus afforded to the contraband 
trader; and were a considerable reduction made in the duty, both the 
revenue and the producer would be gainers; the former in the full 
receipt of its dues, the inducement to smuggling being removed, and the 
latter by an increased demand,

Inclosure 4 in No. 19. 
Mr. Scott to Mr. Consul Moore. 


Sir, Shimlain, June 30, 1846. 


I HAVE the honour to acknowledge receipt of your circular of 24th 
instant, with copy of the letter addressed to you by Her Majesty’s Consul- 
General at Beyrout, respecting the aril of 1839. 

As I am not at present engaged in the import trade of this country, 
I will not trouble you with any observations on that branch of the Tariff, 
the other British merchants, being importers, are more competent to 
furnish you with the necessary information. I however respectfully beg 
to call your attention to the present export duty on silk the produce of 
this country. 

The value of Syrian silk just now ranges from 105 to 115 piastres the 
oke, and | think the present prices may be taken as a fair average for 
other years; the export duty on this silk is fourteen piastres the oke, or 
about 13 per cent. on value, 

I think it may be fairly left to any impartial judgment, whether such 
an exorbitant duty be not unwise and highly impolitic. I am sure it 
will be found that this excessive duty has hitherto only acted as an 
inducement to wholesale smuggling, and has consequently diminished the 
custom-house revenues to a very great extent; indeed this is a fact 
notorious to every resident in Beyrout during the operation of the Tariff 
of 18at, + 

Besides the evil of depriving the Government of the country of a 
revenue, this exorbitant duty is a great hardship to the fair trader, 
whose principles will not allow him to condescend to contraband transae- 
tions; how is it possible for him to compete with the smuggler who has 
such great odds in his favour? 

| have confined my observations to the duty on silk alone, as that is 
the only article in which I am personally interested; but T believe that 
the export duty on the other articles the produce of this country, will also 
be found too high; indeed the valuations formerly made of the produce of 
this country seem to me to have been determined without regard to their 
real value or quality, the valuations of Constantinople and Smyrna 
having been adopted, although the same kind of articles there are very 
superior in quality and value. 

Every wise and enlightened Government has found tts interest in 
rendering the export duty on the produce of the country as light as pos- 
sible, and I hope the members of the Turkish Government and those who 
have influence in the formation of a new tariff for this part of the empire, 
will not fail to perceive the pernicious effects of the existing ‘Tarif! as 
regards the export duty on the produce of the country; and I trust, Sir, 
you will be pleased to oy a to the proper authority the necessity 
there is for a reduction of the export duty, especially as regards silk, 
both as a means of producing more reyenue to the Porte and as a pro- 
tection to the fair trader against the reckless transactions of the smuggler. 

[ have, &e., 
(Signed) JOHN G. SCOTT. 


Inclosure 5 in No. 1. 
Mr. Heald to Mr. Consul Moore. 


Sir, Beyrout, July 27, (846. 

| HAVE the honour to acknowledge your circular of the 24th ultimo, 
inclosing a letter from Colonel Rose, C.B., Her Britannic Majesty's Consul- 
General of Syria, requesting the British merchants within your Consulate 


69 


to furnish statements in detail respecting the several points in which the 
Tariff of 1839 may require amendment; also such observations as they may 
have to offer on the bearings of the existing commercial regulations upon 
British trade with Turkey. 

I have already on former occasions joined with the other merchants 
of this place in eaten of opinion respecting the bearings of the 
existing commercial regulations upon British trade. The principal 
objection to which was, that not being obligatory on Russian subjects 
they gave them an undue advantage, inasmuch as they could both 
import and export goods at a considerable less rate of duty than the 
British trader. 

But as T understand that the Russian Government has accepted 
of or made a ‘Treaty of Commerce with the Turkish Government, similar 
in spirit to the British Treaty, in my opinion all great objections to the 
Treaty are now obviated. : | 
With respect to the Tariff of Customs by which the British merchants 
import and export goods in the Turkish Empire, on the export duty 
I leave it to the export merchants to report, as being’ more competent to 
give an opinion on this part of the Tariff, | 

With reference to the import duties of the Tariff in question, without 
entering into a detailed statement, I am of opinion the whole Tariff 
requires a careful revision, particularly so far as affects the importation of 
British manufactures. 

The full revision of this part of the Tariff is called for by the fact that 
the prices of goods have considerably declined since 1839, on prices of 
whie 1 date the rate of duty was fixed. Again, the quality of the eoods 
imported is much inferior and of a lower value than the description of 
goods imported in 1839; and again, the raw material is much lower than 
in 1880, therefore, from the above consideration, | am of opinion that the 
interest of British merchants requires that a modification of the Tariff of 
import duties on British manufactures should be made, commensurate 
with the change in quality and price of goods and with the alteration in 
currency and exchange which have taken place since the Tariff was 
drawn up. | 

I have, &e., 
(Signed) HENRY HEALD 


Inclosure 6 in No. 19. 


Messrs. Black & Co. to Mr. Consul Moore. 


Sir, Beyrout, July 10, 1846, 

WE have the honour to acknowledge the recept of your cireular letter 
dated 25th ultimo, inclosing copy of a despatch addressed to vou b 
Colonel Rose, Her Britannic Majesty's Consul-General in Syria, directing 
you to obtain from the British merehants within your Consulate, for his 
information, statement in detail respecting the several points in which 
the Tariff of 1839 may require amendment, with any suggestions for im. 
provement, 

On reference to the former representation of the British merchants 
on the subject of the Convention and Tariff of 1838 and 1839, you will 
observe that the great grievance complained of was, the disadyante @ 
under which they laboured as compared with the subjects of Russia. 
But we have reason to believe that this evil has ane been removed 
by a new Convention entered into between Russia and the Porte, whereby 
the subjects of the former are placed on an equality with those of the 
other uropean Powers; and assuming our information to be correct 
we therefore confine ourselves to some observations on the Tarif, 

Since the period when the Tariff of 1839 was constructed, changes 
have occurred to affect the value of the articles both of importation and 
exportation, and render necessary its being remodelled on the basis of the 
now current value. Among these changes the most important one has

Inclosure 1 in No. 30. 


Messrs. Black and Co. to Mr, Consul Werry, 
Sir, Aleppo, June 29, 1846. 


WE have the honour to acknowledge your circular to the British 
merchants here, containing an extract from a cireular from his Excellency 
Sir Stratford Canning, dated Constantinople Sth June, 1846, requesting 
* statements in detail respecting the several points in which the 'Tarill of 
1838 may require amendment.” 

In reply we beg to refer you too report of the merehants here, dated 
24th July, 1$43, to which we appended our signature, As therein stated 
we assented to the opinion that the Tarif’ of 188s had proved beneficial, 
than otherwise, to the British import trade with this country, The mal- 
administration of the custom-house here not unfrequently gives rise to 
disputes and abuses; but these have generally heen rectified at the 
expense of some trouble and loss of time. When the Tariff of 1838 was 
constructed the exchange on England ruled from 160 to 185. piastres por 
pound sterling, and the value of imports in pinstres bunts pondinaly: In 
the latter part of 1843 the Turkish Government reformed its currency, 
thereby reducing the current value of coins about 20 per ecent., and the 
exchange on England to 110 piastres per pound sterling, The currency 
value of imports and exports fell correspondingly, but the customs 
duties have continued to be fevied at Tariff rates, and made payable at 


73 


the new rates of currency. Owing to this and the general reduction in 
the cost and quality of-British imports, the Tariff duties now levied greatly 


exceed the ad valorem rate they were intended to represent. The following 
table of a few of the chief British Imports here will afford an illustration 


of what we state :—

____ From the preceding it is evident that owing to the alteration effected 
in the intrinsic value of the Turkish curreney, which in an equal ratio 
affects the value of merchandise, in piastres, from the inferior quality of 
British fabrics now imported into Syria, and consequently their relatively 
lower price, and from the reduced cost of the articles in the countries 
where they are produced, by which they ean be furnished to the consumer 
bere at a much lower rate; the dutics now exacted are far above what 
was contemplated in forming the Tariff, and the whole import as well as 
export scale of duties requires to be remodelled, on the basis of the current 
value of the articles in this country. : 

As regards exports or produce purchased here for exportation, we 
have still to complain of our disadvantageous position, as compared with 
Russian merchants. rom us the Turkish Government exacts 9 and 3 
equal to 12 per cent, duty; the Russian merchant pays only 3 per cent., 
or a tariffed duty representing that rate; as an instance we may state 
that last year we were permitted as a favour to export sheep's wool 
Bescupbed here, on payment of a duty of 40 piastres per 180 okes; 

ussian merchants at the same time bought and exported the same qualit 
of wool, and paid only 18 piastres per Is) okes, a5 a right, The difler- 
ence in the rates of duty yields a profit; thus competition is out of our 
power, and our general interests are very materially prejudiced thereby, 

_ Phe same applies to almost every description of exports, galls excepted, 
which have hitherto come from the interior and been sold here duty paid, 

The grievance could be éasily remeiicd by putting in foree in Syria 
the same system as we believe’'to be now applied ta Turkey, namely, the 
“9 per cent, in liew of all interior cuties,” to be exacted fram the grower 
or producer, and the purchaser held liable for only the 3 per cent. “export 
duty, In this way the Russian merchant could have little or no advan- 
tage over us, and the Turkish Government could not lose by its 
adoption, 

We remain, &e., 
(Signed) WM. & ROBT. BLACK & Co,

Inclosure 2 in No. 20. 


Messrs. Gibb and Co. to Mr. Consul Werry. 


Sir, | Aleppo, July 1, 1846. 

IN reply to your communication of 24th ultimo, with extract of a 
circular from his Excellency Sir Stratford Canning, relative to those 
points on which the Tarilf of 1838 might require amendment, we beg to 
refer you to the report of the British merchants here, dated 24th July, 
1843 + on which, while we assented to the opinion that the Tanll had 
proved beneficial to the British commerce with this country, we pointed 
out the advantages the Russian merchant had over us in carrying on the 
trade. | ; 

The only other point in which we think the Tariff requires amends 
ment, is such a reyisal of the rates as would bring then again to what 
they were originally intended to represent, namely, an equivalent for an 
ad valorem duty of 5 per cent. on imports, and 12 per cent.on exports, the 
reduction in the exchanges, the difference in the quality and value of 
goods since the Tariff was framed, having completely changed in this 
respect. 

BS We have, &c., 
(Signed) G, H. GIBB & Co.

Inclosure 3 in No, 20, 
Mr. Consul Werry to Sir Stratford Canning. 


Sir, Alenpo, July 4, 1846. 

{1 HAVE the honour to inclose, conformable to the instructions 
conveyed in your Excellency’s circular under date of the 8th June, 
the information obtained [rom our merchants on the subject of the amend- 
ments required in the revision of the ‘Taviii of 1599, and suggestions for 
improvement relating thereto. | et) | 

In the report [ had the honour of transmitting to your Excellency, 
under date of the 3rd August, 1843, 1 submitted for consideration the 
obstacies thrown in the way of trade by the constant contention between 
the custom-houses both on the coast and in the interior, which are for the 
most part farmed, on the validity of the teskérés or retti¢s; these 
have rather increased than diminished, and contrary to the Fourth 
Article of the Convention of 16th August 1838, in which it is stated 
that the custom of 9 and 3 per cent. on produce shall be paid on the 
coast, the 9 per cent. has been levied by the custom-houses in the 
interior, which has occasioned a continual conflict as to the right of the 
internal custom-houses appropriating the same; for instance the custom- 
house here will not accept the teskérés of the custom-houses at Bagdad, 
Moussul, and Diarbekir, from which places goods are not permitted to 
depart unless the 9 per cent, duty is paid, nor will the eustom-house at 
Beyrout accept the teskérés of the custom-house here ; the consequence 
is that the merchants are in constant litigation with the custom-houses, 
their goods detained or ex gonad to pay double duty, or after considerable 

delay ean only obtain redress by application to the authorities at Con- 
stantinople, It would seem that some pone and specilic order is 
required to be issued by the Porte to the internal custom-louses, to 
prevent the contentions on this subject. cca doe 
With regard to the teskérés on imports, the same state of things 
exists as submitted in my last report; the same and greater profits have 
» accrued to the farmers of the customs here, vix., to our present Governor 
Osmun Pasha, and his predecessor Vagich Pasha; by private agreement 
the custom duty has been levied at 4 per cent, during the last year, but 
this year has been reduced to 3 per cent, in licu of & per cent, this 
measure has been adopted to avoid the farmers of the custom-house at 


15 


Beyront conniving with the importers on the payment of a lower rate of 
duty, and thereby granting teskérés to the prejudice of the custom-house 
here; [ know of no measure that can be suggested to your Excellency to 
remedy these infringements of the Convention, so long as good faith does 
not exist between the parties, unless it is by the Porte issuing strong 
orders to the respective custom-houses, and levying a heavy penalty on 
the parties convicted of similar frauds; our merchants will naturally 
guard their interests by compromises with the custom-houses so long as 
they are exposed to the rivalship of persons importing at a lower rate of 
duty, under the sanction of the Russian Treaty and Tariff’; nor can any 
reform be expected from the Turkish revenue ifticers or authorities whose 
malacministration and yenality is not less conspicuous than it has always 
been: there is another point which may be submitted to your Excelleney’s 
consideration, viz., the position in ai htch the custom-houses in the interior 
are situated with the custom-lhouse at Constantinople; if sometimes 
occurs that imports which have been received here a aid the 3 and 2 
per cent. are required, in default of sale or want of a CE to be sent to 
the capital; in such instance the custom-house here refuses to grant a 
teskéré, on the plea that the chief custom-house at Constantinople will not 
recognise the same, and that both the custom-houses are farmed on such 
condition or understanding. 

Our merchants and the trade here generally make eomplaints 
respecting a duty denominated “ Badge,” which is levied on the carriage 
of goods in different places; and although the carriers are made to pay 
this duty, they in consequence compensate themselves by proportionally 
increasing the rate of carriage, which falls on the merchants; between 
this place and Alexandretta two duties are levied, one at Khan Caramout, 
piastres3 12 per load, and another at Beylan, 3.12; the former is taken by 
this Pasha and the latter by the Pasha of Adana, This duty was 
originally levied to guard and repair the roads; but [believe it may be 
safely said that for agreat number of years past there is not asingle guard 
on the road, nor have the roads, brides, or passes, if such they may be 
ealled, ever been repaired by the Government. At Zohi on the Moussul 
road there is a similar duty levied, amounting to 12) piastres per load, 

I submit to your Excellency's consideration whether it may not be 
advisable to remove this imposition, which former amounts annually 
between this place and Alexandretta to about 100,000 piastres, without 
any benefit accruing therefrom, either to the trade or the public. 

| have, &e., 


(Signed)  N. W. WERRY.

Inclosure 2 in No, 21. 


The Merchants of Damascus to Mr, Acting Consul Timoni. 
Monsieur, July 24, 1846. 

EN réponse a votre honor¢e missive du Jer courant, par laquelle 
yous nous demandes des renseignemens sur les points A Végard desquels 
la Convention du 16 Aoiit 1888 saurait étre susceptible d’amendemens, 
et qui couséquemment peuvent avoir en géneral trait a la prosperité du 
commerce Anglais dans cette contrée, nous avons Phonneur, M.le Consul, 
de vous rapporter ce qui suit. : we 

1°. Devons-nous remarquer avant tout, le grand inconvenient que la 
dite Convention renfermait contre les intéréts des négocians Anglais, et 
qui consistait dans la tolérance de la partiale faveur dont pouvaient se 
félicites les sujets et protégés Russes, dans les derniers ans en tout 
Empire Ture; en payant 2 pour cent sur limportation et 9 pour cent 
sur exportation, moins que les autres nations, et en joulssant en indme 
tems de tous les avantages de ces derniers; position favorisée, contre 
laquelle il n’y avait A lutter, tant était-elle onéreuse et humiliante pour 
les non-Russes. 

Nous sommes aises d’apprendre que cet état anomal vient de cesser, 
et nous espérons que cela soit radicalement et pour toujours: car en cas 
contraire et en continuant sur ce pied les sujets Anglais se verront 
totalement expulsés du commerce de leur patrie par des ¢trangers, qui 
le plus souvent ne sont que des protégés et sous-protégés Russes, 
dont le nombre augmente de jour en jour tellement A faire craindre un 
envahissement général, Et cela prouve mieux que tout jusqu’a quel 
degré les dits adversuires sont devenus, par leur position favorisée et 
exceptionnelle, supéricurs aux sujets des autres nations, auxquels dans le 
statu qué ne resterait que la triste alternative, ou de quitter en vaincus 
le champ de bataille, ou de passer en réfugi¢s sous les ailes du tout 
puissant aigle Russe. | i . 

9° Observons-nous qu'il ya aujourd’hui une différence assez sensible 
aux droits fixés dans le Tarif de 1838, relativement 4 la valeur des mar- 
chandises qui, depuis lors, n'ont fait que diminuer de prix en Syrie, de 
sorte que les droits ad valorem ne correspondent plus aux prix presens, 
mais les excédent sensiblement; ce qui fait désirer une révision du Tarif 
en question, et des amendemens en sonesguence eest-d-dire de rélormer 
estimation ad valorem de ces articles dont la valeur a subi une dimi- 
nution depuis la précitée Gpoque. ; Say . 

3°, Serait-il a propos de régler mieux les subdivisions de certains 
articles; comme par exemple les indiennes, les cotons filés, les indigos, 
les draps, les toiles-blanches et éerues, les cafés du ponant, &e., &e., qui 
se trouvent désignés dans l'actuel Tarif, sur une échelle trop limitée ; 


77 


tandis que a cause de leurs différentes qualités, leurs valeurs varient par 
fois de 10 4 30 pour cent, et il n'est: pas raisonnable de payer presque in- 
distinctement les droits de douane de méme sur une mauvaise qualité, 
comme sur une bonne et supérieure. . 
4°, Avait-il derniérement ici de dissensions sur le lieu of devraient 
étre payés les droits de douane; savoir, A l’endroit de l'achat ou A celui 
de lembarcation? Nous optérions pour le premier, du moins pour le 
versement du droit de commutation de 9 pour cent, par le raison qu’é 
Vendroit of on achéte l'article, le méme yaut ordinairement moins et 
n’est pas encore grevé de maintes frais ; ce qui facilite une estimation plus 
basse du méme, et permet au douanier de l'endroit d'exercer souvent une 
certaine connivance. 
Nous avons, &c. 
(Signé) G. TEAEFPINGER. 
G. MARCOPOLL. 
JOSEPH ELIAS.

Inclosure in No, 22. 
Mr. Consul Finn to Sir Stratford Canning. 


Sir, Jerusalem, August 26, 1846. 

HAVING received a circular despatch from Her Majesty's Embassy, 
dated June 8, instructing me to furnish statements in detail respecting 
the several points in which the Tarilf of 1839 may require amendment, 
with such suggestions for improvement as I might be able to obtain from 
British merchants within my district; also to furnish observations of my 
own respecting the bearing of the existing commercial regulations upon 
British trade with Turkey; ; 

I have the honour to report that [ have instituted inquiries on the 
subject among the Consular Agents of Palestine, and from their diffuse 
statements of grievances | gather the following unfortunate facts :— 

1, That on all the sea-coast of Palestine there is but one English 
subject (except the Consular Agent at Saida, who isa Maltese) engaged in 
ecommerce, and he is an Ionian named Mazzinghi, who has one Ranke at 
Caifla and another at Acre. 

2. That of these Consular Agents only the one above mentioned is even 
a British subject, 

3. That in either importation or exportation these and the other 
traders have no communication with England, or with other places for 
English goods, 

4. That such trade as they have is both petty in quality and small in 
amount; it is also diminishing rapidly in eacl oft those respects. 

The statements therefore which [ have received consist principally 
of wrongs suffered by rayah merchants from their local governors in 
their small traffic of 5 bi olive oil, cotton, and sesame, with a

The only dissentient from this desire is the British subject Mazzinghi, 
who recommends the adoption of the Russian duty of 3) per cent. ad 
valorem on all articles of Syrian produce for either importation or 
exportation, ; - 

The Consular Agent at Saida, who is the only other British subject, 
complains that in violation of the Second Article of the Convention the 
Government still retains monopolies of salt, charcoal, and coffee. | 

The complaints of rayah merchants show most forcibly the obstacles 
which impede every gainful commerce in this country, These are well- 
known to arise from the corruption and violence of local mis-government, 
and require no new explanation. It is therefore to be regretted that a 
land with such capacity for inexhaustible agricultural produce, besides 
facility of intercourse with many surrounding nations, should be reduced 
to its present state of humiliation. | 

But [ am convinced that with the ordinary virtues of European 
yeople, especially among the rulers here, a commercial and there 
ore mental and moral improvement would be speedily perceived. And 
the change already produced in this city by the silent habits and the 
requirements of English people since the establishment of the English 
mission to the Jews, and especially of the uae ae as described to me 
by older residents who can observe the change, is an earnest of what 
might be done were the port of Jaffa made more accessible by voltaic 
blasting, were the road restored to order between Jaffa and Jerusalem, 
and had we a British-born Vice-Consul at Jaffa.

IN respectful obedience to instructions which I have received from 
Her Majesty’s Ambassador at Constantinople, I beg leave to submit to 
your Lordship the inclosed copy of a report which | have this day made 
to his Excelleney respecting the several points in which the Tariff of 1839 
requires amendment, with my suggestions for its Improvement, together 
with some observations respecting the bearing of the existing commercial 
regulations upon British trade in Turkey.

Inclosure | in No, 23. 


Mr, Consul Kerr to Sir Stratford Canning. 


British Consulate of Cyprus, 
Excellency, Larnaca, July 4, 1846. 


1 HAVE had the honour of receiving your Excelleney’s circular 
despatch dated the 8th ultimo, desiring me te furnish statements in detail 
respecting the several points in which the Tariff of 1839 may require 
amendment, with suggestions for WABrOveRIe Rs and I shall not fail 
forthwith to transmit to the Foreign Office a copy of this despatch, in 
obedience to your Excellency’s instructions, 

I beg to inclose herewith a list of the duties which would be payable 
on the articles of exportation from Cyprus, under our existing Taruf, 
provided they had been rated at their respective market prices, 


79 


It will be seen thereby what serious injury our commerce now sullers, 
and the heavy duty with which it is surcharged, owing to the rates esta- 
blished in 1839. 

I merely illustrate this assertion by referring your Excellency to my 
despatch on this same subject, No, 13, dated 3lst December, in which 1t 
is stated that olive oil now pays 134, colocynth 15, cotton and wool 163, 
printed calicoes 25, common red wine 42, and Commanderia wine 72 per 
cent., instead of 12 per cent, as stipulated in the Commercial Convention, 
begging that your Excellency will be kindly pleased to direct that steps 
may be taken to deliver our commerce from these heavy burthens through 
the medium of our new ‘Tariff. 

I beg respectfully to express to your Excellency my strong conviction 
that our trade in Cyprus, and throughout the Levant in general, must 
continue in a comparatively unfavourable position until the First Article 
of our Commercial Treaty of 1835 is brought te bear with all its force, 

The idea of subdividing the 12 per cent. export duty, leaving 9 per 
cent. thereof chargeable to the grower, and 3 per cent. to the British 
merchant on the shipment of his purchases, may be just in theory, but it 
is illusive in practice. 

Should a British subject desire to clear for exportation at the custom- 
house 1000 kintals of carubs, the customer demands of him 12 per cent., 
making the duty for which he is liable amount to 1650 piastres; but of a 
Russian clearing the same goods, only 3 per cent. is claimed, making 
4124 piastres, one quarter of the amount which the Englishman is com- 
pelled to pay. 

We are however informed in the latter case, that the rates of the 
Russian Tariff are assimilated to those adopted by the English, French, 
and Austrians, and that the custom-house has merely to apply to the 
rower to encash the other three-quarters, and that that is the same 
thing. 

The Russian however is in no way compelled to declare the name of 
his seller; but let us suppose he declares him to he a French or Spanish 
subject, the custom-house might claim of either party 1297) piastres as a 
balance due to it on goods which had been sold without any idea of their 
being intended for exportation. The custom-house might then fall 
back on another seller, and then a new question arises as to whether it is 
obligatory to indicate him, or even the goods may have been previously 
purchased in small lots of various sellers, so that it might be impossible 
to declare them. 

I believe it is universally admitted that our Commercial Convention 
of 1838, if it weighed impartially on all nations, would not only be a great 
advantage to European commerce, but a still greater to Turkey; the 
abolition of manele in the Levant has invigorated trade, and begins to 
animate agriculture, } . 

If the Porte owed no greater debt of oblixation to Great Britain than 
this, it would be difficult indeed forittorepay her, ‘The Porte ought to have 
perceived that the friendly counsels which when offered were only repug- 
nantly accepted, have already brought forth their good fruits; it ought 
now therefore to oppose no difficult y to placing all the Kuropean Powers, 
and among them England, on a footing of perfect commercial equality, 
and leaving the development of the rich cloments of prosperity which 
lie hidden and unexplored in its provinees, to the most unrestricted 
competition. 

Should the Turkish Government now pursue an opposite line of 
policy it will result that all European merchants will seck (as many have 
already done), the Russian protection, or by the payment of a commission 
of Lor 2 per cent, they will secure to their operations the safeguard of 
Russia, so that all the Levant trade will become tributary to that Power, 

In order therefore to insure our being placed on the same footing as 
Russia, it is absolutely necessary that an Article of our new Convention 
should clearly and without any restriction, establish that the British 
merchant shall have in future the right of claiming and availing himself, 
de fucto, of all the privileges, favours, or facilities, that have, or at any 
future period may be granted to the most favoured nation. 


Lh es 


80 


If at the present day well-organised Governments admit the necessity 
of reducing their 'Tarifls with a view of pag their commerce, how 
much more is such a policy needful in Turkey, where agriculture ought 
to be encouraged, and where commerce is but in its infancy ? 

When England suggested to the Porte the abolition of monopolies 
the latter immediately sought a compensation, with a view to which the 
9 per cent. duty was fixed, in addition to the duty of 3 per cent. which 
previously existed, | ; \ heen: 

Would it not be possible at this day, when the Divan is enlightened 
by more experience, to advise it entirely to renounce the o per cent, which 
sustains an organized system of contraband, whieh demoralizes the 
subaltern agents, which produces no real increase to the finances, and 
which paralyzes the agriculturist ? 

| Ee to submit to your Excellency, as a practical argument, that 
during the past year, 1545, only a fifth part of the silk exported from 
Cyprus was cleared at the custom-house, and we are well aware that 
on the coasts of Syria an equally extensive system of smuggling is 
earried on. ” 

A simple export duty of 3 per cent., which no one would attempt to 
elude, because smugeling would be more expensive, would produce re 
steady and flourishing revenue to the Turkish treasury ; the agricultural 
interest and trade in general would thereby receive an immense impetus, 
and the Sultan would have the satisfaction of having by these means 
increased the welfare of his subjects. 

[ am well aware that it would be difficult to convinee the Turkish 
Government of the wisdom of following out a principle which begins by a 
temporary sacrifice of revenue ; but when we see the first statesmen of the 
age, after many years’ experience, proclaim these principles, Great 
Britain could with great confidence imvite others to follow her steps, 
because she proves to them by her own example the sincerity of her 
counsels, ae 

Having been desired by your Excellency to submit my opinions rela- 
tive to our existing Tariff with the Porte, | should consider that I had not 
performed my duty had 1 not unreservedly laid before your Excellency 
my humble views on the subject; and I trust that the circumstance of my 
having been several years engaged in the Turkey trade may probably 

lead my excuse for entering so fully into details. 

I flatter myself, however, that if 1 have involuntarily enlarged on the 
question on which your Excellency has been pleased to order me to 
express my opinion, your Excellency will kindly place a favourable 
construction on my intentions. 

I have, &e., 
(Signed) NIVEN KERR, 
Consul.

Mr, Consul Ongley to Viscount Palmerston.—(Received September 28.) 
(No. 12.) 


My Lord, Canea in Crete, August 19, 1846. 

I HAVE the honour to transmit inclosed copy of a statement sent by 
me to Her Majesty’s Ambassador at Constantinople, relative to the present 
Tariff rates of duties on goods imported into and exported from Turkey, 
as well as remarks on the present commercial relations. 

_ With respect to imports, I have confined myself to a few articles 
which are not generally imported into other parts of the empire. 


ee 


j 
| 
i 


82 


In the exports a very great difference will be seen between the 
proposed rate and present rate of duty, particularly on oil, soap, and 
wool, 

Since I forwarded this statement | have seen a translation of the new 
Russian Commercial Treaty; by the Sixth Article of it, the retail trade, 
as well as the manufacturing trade (under which term is included, | 
apprehend, in the Turkish original word, almost every sort of manual art 
and labour) is prohibited to Russian subjects. 

Were such a clause inserted in our ‘Treaty, it would have the effect 
of throwing out of employment an immense number of Tonians and 
Maltese. 

In this island, almost the whole of the retail trade in imports is in 
the hands of foreigners, and of the artisans, the greatest part are Ionians, 
Hellenes, or Maltese. 


Were these persons prohibited from exercising their calling, how | 


would the Turks replace them? The want of population in this island, 
as well asin other parts of Turkey, is abundantly evident, and it would 
be very impolitic for the Sublime Porte to adopt a measure which it may 
consider to be a concession on the part of Russia, but which is in reality, 
detrimental to the real interest of ‘Turkey. 

The only advantage which could accrue to Turkey from prohibiting 
foreigners to engage in the retail trade, or to exercise any art or engage 
in any manufacture would be, that many Iellenes might become Ottoman 
subjects for the purpose of being allowed to exercise their calling. 

By the ‘Tenth Article the importation of cannon, powder, &e. 1s 
prohibited, whilst at the same time it is provided that persons may sell 
shot and powder in proportion; but how are they to sell the powder if they 
are not allowed to import it? 

[ think it would be more suitable to place powder on the same 
footing that cannon are to be placed by this Article, that is, that it shall 
be warchoused in the Government stores, and sold only in small quantities 
with the knowledge of the local authorities. 

By the first section of the Eleventh Article, fishing and the sale of 
fish is reserved to Turkish subjects; this will have the effect of rendering 
the article very dear in the island, and will thus deprive the poorer 
inhabitants of a great luxury. 

Neapolitans, [onians, and Maltese catch the greatest part of the fish 
consumed here. 

The third section of the same Article subjects the sale of alum to 
certain restrictions. [am quite ata loss to understand why the Turkish 
authorities should wish to interfere with the sale of this article so 
essential in dyeing, The fourth section contains an admission that the 
importation of salt is prohibited; a measure, of which most Europeans in 
Turkey have hitherto, | imagine, been ignorant. 

It also contains a provision to send Turkish salt from one district to 
another,—a most prejudicial measure for this island, which imports 
largely from Barbary, as this article is absolutely necessary for making 

oap. 

The 5th and 7th sections are also objectionable. 

In short, I consider the tenor of this 11th Article prejudicial to trade 
and to the real interest of Turkey, suffering as it does from the want of 
population to cultivate its extensive plains. 

To facilitate a reference to the Articles of the Russian Treaty to which 
I allude, 1 bee to inclose a French translation of it. 

Trusting that your Lordship will excuse the liberty | have taken in 
making these remarks, 

1 have, &e., 
(Signed) Hl, 8. ONGLEY,
