Remarks. 


lat. Difficulties frequently arise with respect to the value of those 
roods which pay ad valorem, and the custom-house authorities pretend 
that the selling price is the value on which the duty is to be calculated. 


Y 


Se an 


84 


This, so far as regards importations, is unjust. It would be advisable to 
come to an understanding that the value on which the duty is to be cal- 
culated is to be the selling market price, less 10 or 15 per cent, or the 
duty to be paid in kind, It would not be advisable to take the invoice 
cost as a basis, because many parties would present fictitious invoices. 

2nd, No provision exists in the ‘Treaty, relative to the duties on 
Turkish produce imported into Turkey from a British possession. We 
have imported from Malta ‘nto this island the produce of Egypt and the 
Barbary States. 

When the produce is exported from this island to Turkey, the duty 
exacted is the same as if the exportation were made to Europe, except 
that 10 per cent. on the amount of duty is also exacted under the name of 
“Kalemyié” (office money); and a certificate of the duties having been 
paid is given to the shipper, so as to protect him from the exaction of any 
other duty at the port of discharge; but no certificate is given, nor 
“ Kalemyié ” exacted, if the goods be shipped for Kurope. 

If Turkish produce arrive here, and the importer be not provided 
with the above-mentioned certificate, the 9 and 3 per cent. duties, plus 
13 per cent. on their amount, is exacted, . 

A party shipping from Barbary to Malta a cargo of barilla, with the 
intention of selling it at Malta, does not provide himself with a certificate 
of the duties having been paid in Barbary. This may be sold at Malta, 
to a party who ships it to this island. 

n its arrival here, it is recognised as Turkish produce ; and being 
unprovided with a certificate of the duties having been paid in Barbary, 
12 per cent. and the additional 10 per cent. on the amount of the duty is 
exacted here; whereas. the shipper at Malta probably supposed he would 
have to pay only 5 per cent. as an importation direct from a British pos- 
session, thus making a difference of more than 7 per cent. in his calcula- 
tions. 

If, however, the importer were provided with acertifieate of the duties 
having been paid in Barbary, no duties whatsoever would be exacted 
here, unless the goods imported were the produce of the Kingdom of 
‘Tunis. 

{ would suggest that Turkish produce imported through a British 
ossession, if provided with a certifieate that the duties had been duly 
evied at the original place of export, should pay no duty on entering 

Turkey again, but if provided with no such certificate, should pa 
3 and 2 per cent. at the port of discharge, as if it were of British 
production. 

Sed. In the new Russian Commercial Treaty with the Porte, if I am 
rightly informed, some mono yalies are consented to; and it is also stipu- 
lated that Russian subjects shall not have the nght of engaging in manu- 
factures in ‘Turkey, it would in my opinion not be advisable toadmitsuch a 
clause in our Treaty. 

Many manufactures may be earried on or perfected by British 
eapital and sd ado to the advantage of Turkey, without any detriment 
to British trade. 

The Turkish Governors are in general disposed to impose taxes on 
every article with the direct sanction of the central Government, and thus 
discourage individuals from carrying on manufacturing adventures, as 
they dare not make representations to the Porte, whereas if Europeans 
be allowed to manufacture they will oppose any arbitrary exaction of the 
Local Governors, and be thus a salutary check on them without any 
detriment to the Porte, and were any impolitic restriction to be placed on 
their industry by the Central Government, they would endeavour to 


cbtain its revecation by pointing out its mjurious tendency, whereas 


native manufacturers would either be unable or alraid to do it. 

1 may mention silk-winding, soap-making, and converting wheat 
into flour by steam, as some of the adventures in which British capital 
are or might be embarked, 

4th, ‘The present high rate of duties on exports has had the effect 
of throwing a great part ef the trade into the hands of those parties 
who make no difficulty of defrauding the revenue, | would venture to sug- 


Bo 


hl that the Porte be recommended to increase the penalty now exacted 
rom those detected in smuggling transactions. 

At present the only penalty is the payment of double duty ; whereas 
were it increased to forfeiture of the article seized, the risk would be too 
great in proportion to the duty, and rt may reasonably be supposed that 
smuggling would then in a great measure decrease. 

‘he master of the vessel when assisting in the smuggling ought also 
to be subjected to a fine. 

Till some steps are taken in this respect, British merchants cannot 
compete with foreigners, 

The Pasha here by consenting to put a low valuation on silk, and by 
giving to the custom-house officers whatever double duty might be 
exacted on the silk they might seize, has very greatly increased the 
quan entered at the custom-house; and I feel convinced that if the 
ine exacted were the full value of the silk, instead of only double duty, 
smugeling here would almost entirely cease. 

Sth. it would perhaps he advisable to come to some clear understand- 
ing pseperting the right of British subjects to enter into the retail trade 
in Turkey.

ARTICLE I. 


Tous leg traités et stipulations qui ont existé jusqu’ici entre la Sub-

ui est expressément aboli ou modifié par Ja présente Convention, La

autorités militaires de terre et de mer et autres fonctionnaires. Kile 
promet et assure en outre aux sujets et bitimens Russes la pleine et 
enti¢re jouissance de tout droit, bénélice, ou avantage, qui est ou serait 
accordé dorénayant dans ses Etats aux nations étrangéres les plus 
favorisées.

de Vindustrie de la Turquie, soit pour les exporter, sort pour en faire le

Les sujets Russes ou leurs hommes d'affaires auront le droit de trans-

ARTICLE IV. 


Tout ohjet produit du sol ou de l'industrie de la Russie ou des pays 
étrangers, qui appartiendrait A des sujets Russes, sera admis comme par 
le passé dans toutes les parties de l’Empire Ottoman, moyennant le paie- 
ment de 3 pourcentdedroitd’importation. En remplacement de tout droitet 
redevances intérieures qui out pesé sur les cits objets, le négociant Russe

Lesdeux Cours Contractantes prenant en considération que parmi les

ARTICLE XVII. 


Les droits et les dispositions stipulées par la présente Conyention a 
Hégard des sujets et négocians Russes ne pouvant pas, (apres. les lois 
commerciales observées en Russie, étre enti¢rement appliquées dans les

le Tarif

Inclosure 2 in No. 25. 


The Merchants of Tripoli to Mr. Consul-General Warrington. 
(Translation.) 


Sir, Tripoli, Barbary, July 30, 1846. 

THE undersigned merchants, British subjects, having by previous 
applications informed you of the abuses and ageravations of this custom- 
house, do now hear with pleasure that Her Britannie Majesty's Ambas- 
sador at Constantinople has taken great interest for the suppression of the 
same; and therefore they beg you will be pleased to submit to his Excel- 
leney at Constantinople this frets present information, which contains a 
résumé of what they have stated before. 


Exportations. 


1. Yellow skins (called bazanes), hides, goat and sheep-skins, bar- 
racans, carpets, &e., which are purchased by the British merchants into 
the town, are they to pay the duty of 3 per cent. on exportation as usual 
(and as it appears by a certificate from all other merchants, dated 13th 
January, 1645) or more, or as the officers of the custom-house pre- 
tend ? 

2, Gall-nuts are sold here at from 60 to 65 piastres per weiba of about 
2) cantars; pays duty 10 piastres and 32 paras, which comes to 17 or 18 
per cent. 

3. Red pepper is sold at 30 piastres per weiba ( which weighs 20 
okes), here it pays the very heavy duty of 25 per cent.; 1! the regular 
duty of 12 per cent. were to be paid, we only ought to pay 3 plastres: per 
weiba, 

4, Senna. The price current here is 175 piastres per cantar, conform- 
able to the 12 per cent.; it ought to pay 21 piastres, but the custom-house 
here takes 254 piastres. per cantar. 

5. Klephant's tecth (or ivory). Notwithstanding the custom-house 
receiving the 12 per cent. in kind, there always arise questions, because 
they wish to choose the best, which causes great damage, and the assort- 
ment is spoiled. 

We must observe that all articles coming from Soudan, ‘Timbuctoo, 
and other places in Nigritia into this Ottoman dominion, viz. in Fezzan, 
paya duty of 9 per cent. in ready cash, without eetting any teskeéré or 
other document stating that the article has paid at Feazan the 9 per cent. 
already ; and when those articles reach Tripoli they make the Arabs pay 
asecond customs’ duty of 13 per cent., prohibiting them to sell those 
articles to Europeans free of duty; in consequence the second duty falls 
on ae British merchants, as it is included in the price of the article so 
sold, 


Importations. 


Wheat, corn, barley, &c., which they pretend to be the produce of 
Turkey, on coming here from Europe pay 12 per cent, custom-house 
duty; they also insist to get the 12 per cent. on all articles in general the 
produce of Tunis coming from Malta or Europe ; whereas we intend to 
pay only the 5 per cent, as on all other articles from Europe, according: to 
the Tariff of 1839, 

Coarse linen pays 40} paras per oke, each piece of 24 yards weighing 
less than 2 okes; by calculating it pays 64 (instead of the 5) per cent, as 
by the "Tarif, | 

Paper. threemoons, pays customs 30 piastres for each bale of 12 
reams; according to the Tariff it should pay 12 and not 20 piastres. 

Soup pays custom 10 piastres per cantar, it ought to pay 54 piastres 
as by the Tariff, 

Sugar in loaves pays duty 14 piastres per cent.: according to the 
Tariff it ought to pay no more than 9. 


93 


Fine sugar crushed pays customs 12 piastres per cantar; it ought by 
the Tariff to pay 6 piastres and 36 paras. oa 

Brown sugar pays duty 8 piastres per cantar ; by the Tariff it should 
pay 5 piastres 4 paras. AG a 

Pack-thread pays duty 20 piastres per cent. ; according to the Tariff 
it ought to pay 7). 

The foregoing are the articles most important and on which com- 
merce suffers a great deal, leaving aside those of less consequence. 

We have, &c., 


(Signed in the Italian by fourteen merchants, all British subjects.)

Me mee Esa DeCe to the bearing of the existing commercial regulations 
upan ae trade with ‘Purkey, it is universally admitted that the 
Bie yal 838 has worked extremely well, and produced results most 
ie uly benelicial to the trade both of this country and of Great Britain 
Bs : re eeDs the cultivators of the soil from the oppression exercised 
over them by monopolists, the exportation of produce to England bas 
been more than doubled in the short space of six years. 5 
” a 1839, ninety-one vessels, mensuring 15,000 tons, were loaded for 
the United Kingdom. Since that period the number has heen Inereasing 


yearly; and in 1845 no less tl ( faring. :

95 


No complaints have of late years been made to me by any British 
subjects, of infractions of the Treaty by the Turkish authorities in this 
district, who are very cautious in their conduct towards Europeans and 
their agents. 

or the first four years after it came into operation, no other duties 
than the tythe and the 9 per cent. were levied; but in 1843 the Porte 
re-established one of the old duties, called “ Iktissabié,” which had been 
abolished, as well as many others, 1n 1839. 

The intention of the Porte was, that this duty should be raised on 
articles sold for home consumption only, and it was fixed by a firman at 
rates equal to 24 per cent. on the value, These were not at first exceeded, 
but not long after the promulgation of the order, the local Governors, 
having themselves become the farmers of the duty, began to increase it; 
and they now exact as much as 8 per cent. from the Ottoman sellers of 
produce of every deseription, whether it be bought for consumption in the 
country or for exportation. 

This abuse may be carried to any extent, as the authorities in the 
interior are not subject to any controul, and no attention has been paid by 
the Porte to the complaints of the sufferers by the rapacity and extortion 
of its subordinates. 

The Treaty of 1838 is infringed, and its object defeated by these pro- 
ceedings, as the 9 per cent. internal duty, which is levied on all goods 
consumed in ‘Turkey, as well as on those exported, was substituted for all 
other interior duties (except the tythe), and as its provisions are declared 
to be “applicable to all the subjects of the Ottoman dominions,” they are 
not, [ conceive, liable ta pay any other duties on the goods they sell, 
whether consumed in ‘Turkey or not. 

The framers of the Treaty justly considered that if the Turkish 
Government received 22 per cent. duty on the produce of the soil exported, 
and 19 per cent. on that consumed in the country, that it ought to be 
satisfied: and iL was assuredly on the understanding that no more 
should be paid, that the internal duty was fixed at 9 per cent., with 
a view to encourage and extend agriculture and trade, by relieving them 
from the oppressive burdens by which they had been overwhelmed, anc 
thereby to increase the prosperity of the empire, which however will again 
be reduced to its former wretched state, if fresh taxes be imposed, and the 

‘ntroduction of abuses be connived at by the Porte, 

in Article ILE of the recent Russian Treaty, this duty (Llktissabié) 
is particularly mentioned, and its suppression 15 distinctly announced, 

Article IV of the Convention of 1835 provides that the 9 per 
cent. duty shall be paid “in lieu of all other interior duties ;" and it 
appears to me that if the words, “ whether levied on the producer, seller, 
or purchaser, which duties are hereby absolutely abolished,” were added 
to them, the meaning would be rendered so precise and clear as to leave 
the Porte no pretext for continuing to levy the Iktissabié, or of im- 
posing any other duty in fature on the score of misinterpretation, 

lt is stipulated in Article 1V of the recent Russian Treaty, that the 
$ per cent. import duty and the 2 per cent, additional duty shall be 
paid at once, or the latter in a year from the date of entry under 
ruurantes, 

The British merchants whom | have consulted on this point are of 
opinion that it would be advisable to accede to a similar arrangement 
with the Porte, for as the 2 per cent. city is not payable so long as 
goods remain unsold in the hands of the importer, facilities are thereby 
afforded to persons so inclined, of evading it altogether, to the great pre- 
judice of the fair trader and of the revenue. Tn fact a large portion of it 
is lost to the collector, 

[f this alteration were made it should be accompanied by this indis- 
pensable condition, that the ‘Turkish Government gunrantee the restitution 
of this duty, on shipment toa foreign country of the goods on which it 
may have ae paid, to a farmer of the customs who might be out of 


office when such shipment took place, as his successor would of course 
refuse to reimburse the shipper a duty which he had not received, unless 
hound to do so by his contract with the Porte,

In the recent Treaty Russia has formally prohibited her subjects 
from carrying on any trade in retail, or from participating in the traffe 
of the esnafs or corporations, and has declared that the exercise of all 
handieraft trades belong exclusively to the subjects of the Porte. 

This has been viewed with some alarm by the Llonian subjects esta- 
blishéd at this place; and the manufacturers of wine and spirits, who 
are very extensively concerned in this branch of trade, and are the most 
influential among them, being apprehensive that the Porte, on the revision 
of our Tariff, will endeavour to obtain similar concessions from Her / 
Majesty's Government, have addressed me a memorial on this subjects SS Se we Es in en | De en a

Memorial of Ionidn Tavérn-Keepers'to Mr. Consul Brimt. 


M, le Consul, Sinyrne, le 5 Janvier, 1847. 

LES Soussignés, sujets loniens, chefs des taverniers (fabricans de 
vins) dans cette ville, ayant appris que sous peu le nouyeau tarif des 
droits de la douane va étre réglé entre l'Angleterre et la Turquie, et 
comme cette derniére Puissance pourrait profiter d'une telle occasion 
pour apporter des changemens ou faire des restrictions au Traité de 
Commerce de lfannée 1838, en se repliant sur les concessions quielle a 
récemment obtenues du Gouvernement Russe, au moyen de la Convention 
du 4 Avril, 1846. Les Soussignés, tout en nourissant la confiance la plus 
étendue dans‘la clairvoyance et le aéle des autorités Britanniques ans 
ce pays, pour la liberté du commerce, et pour la prospérité en général 
de leurs administrés, ne peuvent néanmoins se dispenser de vous soumettre 
jue leurs intéréts se trouveraient gravement compromis par une modt- 
fication quelconque A V’état actuel des choses; et a ces fins ils prennent 
la liberté de vous faire les observations suivantes -— 

La Russie en adhérant aux conditions de son dernier Traité de Com- 
merce avee la Sublime Porte, reconnait formellement que ses sujets ne 
doivent pas faire part des esnafs ou corporations des différentes branches 
industrielles dans la Turquie, et par cette clause elle a eu en vue un 
double but: 1°, celui d’avoir Pair de favoriser l'industrie nationale de la 
Turquie, au détriment de ses propres sujets, tandis qu’elle, ne permettant 
pas aux véritables sujets Russes de quitter leur pays, il n'y en a pas un 
seul qui soit établi en Turquie, et ceux qui passent sous ce nom ne sont 
réellement que des Rayas de la Porte qui ont acheté la nationalité Russe, 
et n'appartient qu’a la classe des négocians, car les moyens limités des 
artisans et des cultivateurs des terres ne leur permet pas de se rendre 
en Russie pour aequérir cette nationalité-lA, et pour contribuer aux 
redeyances annuelles qui y sont attachées. 2°, Parceque la Russie, dans 
ses vues par trop connues d'une politique hostile, a voulu par les susdites 
concessions porter atteinte aux relations de la Turquie avec les autres 
Puissances MBarope qui ont des nationaux établis dans ces contrées, 
et qui de tems immémorial appartient aux classes industriclles du pays 
et font partie des esnafs. Ce quwau commencement était une talérance 
du Gouvernement Ottoman, devint un droit fondé sur la coutume non- 
interrompue de longues années. Et le Gouvernement Ottoman lnvorisa 
ce droit pareeque il y trouva son compte, ses propres sujets n étant pas, 
comme ils ne le sont pas aujourd’hui, 4 méme d’exploiter le vaste domaine 
de Vindustrie indigéne, we. Cee 

Dans la jouissance de ee droit coutumier, la Grande Bretagne, plus 
que toute autre Puissance Furopéenne, y est interessee, car les Anglais 
et les Ioniens, soit par leur nombre, soit par Vimportance et | étendue de 
leurs ¢tabligsemens industrielles, sont précisément ceux qui embrassent 
une plus large échelle; par exemple, dans la corporation des Paverniers, 
les neuf-dixiémes sont des sujets loniens; ces mémes sujets, sur les 
garanties que leur offrait le Traité de 1838, ont sensiblement augmente 
ae ateliers et étendu leurs propriétés y relatives. Ce qui est dit par 
rapport aux Taverniers, dans les proportions moins grandes, on peut dire 
de tous les autres arts et métiers, tant de ceux of la matidre primitive 
est du produit du sol Ture, que de celle qui est importée de lextérieur,
